Nonlinear PDEs with modulated dispersion II: Korteweg-de Vries equation
Khalil Chouk, Massimiliano Gubinelli, Guopeng Li, Jiawei Li, and Tadahiro Oh

Abstract
. We study dispersive equations with a time non-homogeneous modulation acting on the linear dispersion term. As primary models, we consider the Korteweg-de Vries equation (KdV) and related equations such as the Benjamin-Ono equation (BO) and the intermediate long wave equation (ILW), imposing certain irregularity conditions on the time non-homogeneous modulation. In this work, we establish phenomena called regularization by noise in three-folds: (i) When the modulation is sufficiently irregular, we show that the modulated KdV on both the circle and the real line is locally well-posed in the regime where the (unmodulated) KdV equation is known to be ill-posed. In particular, given any s ∈ ℝ s\in\mathbb{R} , we show that the modulated KdV on the circle with a sufficiently irregular modulation is locally well-posed in H s ​ ( 𝕋 ) H^{s}(\mathbb{T}) . For example, this result implies that if the modulation is given by a fractional Brownian motion with Hurst index 0 < H < 2 3 0<H<\frac{2}{3} , the modulated KdV on the circle is locally well-posed in H − 1 2 ​ H + ε ​ ( 𝕋 ) H^{-\frac{1}{2H}+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}) for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . Moreover, by adapting the I I -method to the current modulated setting, we prove global well-posedness of the modulated KdV in negative Sobolev spaces. (ii) It is known that certain (semilinear) dispersive equations such as BO and ILW exhibit quasilinear nature. We show that sufficiently irregular modulations make the modulated versions of these equations semilinear by establishing their local well-posedness by a contraction argument, providing local Lipschitz continuity of the solution map. (iii) We also prove nonlinear smoothing for these modulated equations, where we show that a gain of regularity of the nonlinear part becomes (arbitrarily) larger for more irregular modulations. As applications of our approach, we also include the following examples: (iv) deep-water and shallow-water convergence of the modulated (scaled) ILW in arbitrary negative Sobolev spaces. (v) the modulated KdV on the circle with white noise initial data. (vi) stochastic modulated KdV on the circle with an additive space-time white noise and singular additive noises of arbitrarily low regularity.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60H15, 35Q53, 60H50, 35Q35, 60L20

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Modulated dispersive equations

In this paper, we study a modulated dispersive equation of the following form:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+Lu\cdot\partial_{t}w=\mathcal{N}(u)\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M},

(1.1)

where

\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
,
1
1

1

By convention, we endow

\mathbb{T}
with the normalized Lebesgue measure
dx_{\mathbb{T}}=(2\pi)^{-1}dx

such that we do not need to carry factors involving
2\pi
.

u:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unknown,
the operator
L
denotes an (unbounded) linear dispersive operator,
and
\mathcal{N}(u)
denotes the nonlinearity.
Here,

w:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is an arbitrary continuous function of time,
called a
modulation
,
which will play a central role in our analysis.
Previously,
de Bouard and Debussche
[
41
]
and Debussche and Tsutsumi
[
45
]

used stochastic calculus to study
the modulated nonlinear Schrödinger equations (NLS) with the modulation
w
given by a Brownian motion;
see also
[
49
,
128
]
.
For a general modulation
w
,
such an approach based on stochastic calculus,
interpreting (
1.1
)
as an Ito or Stratonovich stochastic PDE
is not available, and our goal in this paper is to develop a
pathwise
approach
to study the modulated dispersive equation (
1.1
),
exploiting
irregularity
of the modulation function
w
;
see Definition
1.1
.
In the previous work

[
28
]
, the first two authors developed a pathwise
approach to study
well-posedness of the modulated NLS.
In this paper, we study
the following modulated
Korteweg-de Vries equation (KdV)
on both the circle and the real line:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{2}

(1.2)

as our primary model example.
The (unmodulated) KdV equation:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u=\partial_{x}u^{2},

(1.3)

derived as a model for uni-directional shallow water waves,
is of fundamental importance
in mathematics and has been studied extensively
from both the applied and theoretical viewpoints.
The modulated KdV (
1.2
) naturally appears
as a model
for
weakly nonlinear long waves in an inhomogeneous waveguide;
see
[
30
,
76
]
, where
the modulation
w
is taken to be periodic but not differentiable.
There is, however, no analytical study on the modulated KdV (
1.2
)
and we hope to make an important first step in this direction.
We also study the related modulated dispersive equations,
appearing in the study of fluid motion,
such as

•

modulated mKdV equation (
1.13
),

•

modulated Benjamin-Ono equation (
1.16
),

•

modulated intermediate long wave equation (
1.17
),

•

stochastic modulated KdV equation with an additive forcing (
1.30
).

Our main motivation to study the modulated dispersive equation of
form (
1.1
)
is
to underline the regularization effect of a non-homogeneous time modulation,
in the spirit of the work of Flandoli, Priola, and the second author on the stochastic transport equation;
see
[
52
]
.
The so-called
regularization by noise

phenomenon
has been observed for different models:

•

many results in the SDE case since the 70’s

[
134
,
132
,
90
,
38
,
35
,
19
,
36
]
,

•

stochastic parabolic equations

[
66
,
48
,
17
]
,
linear transport equations with rough drifts
[
52
]
,
scalar conservation laws
[
27
]
,
Hamilton-Jacobi equations
[
94
]
,

•

regularization by non-random noises:

[
58
,
53
,
126
]
.

See
[
126
]
for further references.
We refer interested readers to survey works
[
51
,
62
]
.

We point out, however, that rather few results
on regularization by noise are known
for dispersive equations, mainly limited to random initial data / additive noises of super-critical regularity;
see Remark
1.8
.
In this paper, we carry out pathwise analysis to establish several forms of regularization by noise
for canonical dispersive equations
such as the KdV and BO equations.
See the subsequent subsections
in this introduction
for concrete examples.

The presence of a modulation
w
in (
1.1
) prevents
a straightforward use of the Fourier restriction norm method
due to Bourgain
[
6
]
,
thus forcing us
to develop an alternative approach based on nonlinear Young integration as in
[
28
]
.
See Section
3
,
where we go over a general theory of nonlinear Young integrals
and well-posedness of a Young differential equation.
We point out that
the nonlinear Young integral approach
we develop here
has
some similarity
with
the refinement of the Fourier restriction norm method
due to Koch and Tataru
[
87
]
,
where an ‘endpoint Young integral’ via the
U^{2}
-
V^{2}
duality
plays a crucial role.
Lastly, we mention recent works

[
123
,
130
,
124
,
125
]
,
where our pathwise approach via nonlinear Young integrals
has been applied to various modulated dispersive equations.

1.2.
Irregularity of the modulation

At this stage,
the modulated dispersive equation (
1.1
)
is merely formal, since the time derivative of the modulation
w
does not exist in general.
One of our primary interests
is to consider the case where
w
is a sample path of a stochastic process
such as a Brownian motion and, more generally, a fractional Brownian motion.

To bypass this problem,
we will interpret the equation (
1.1
) in terms of the
Duhamel formulation (= mild formulation):

u(t)=U^{w}(t)u_{0}+U^{w}({t})\int_{0}^{t}(U^{w}({t^{\prime}}))^{-1}\mathcal{N}(u(t^{\prime}))dt^{\prime},

(1.4)

where
U^{w}(t)=e^{-w(t)L}
denotes the modulated linear propagator
and we impose
w(0)=0
such that

U^{w}(0)=\Id
.
2
2

2

The normalization
w(0)=0

is not an additional restriction since only the time derivative
\partial_{t}w

appears in the modulated equation (
1.1
).

Note that the Duhamel formulation (
1.4
)
only involves the modulation
w
but not its derivative,
allowing us to study the problem
even when
w
is only continuous.
The aim of this paper is to study
the Duhamel formulation (
1.4
) under
the assumption on the
irregularity
of the modulation
w

introduced in
[
19
,
28
]
;
see Definition
1.1
.
In particular,
for the modulated KdV (
1.2
),
we will show that if
w
is sufficiently irregular,
then (
1.4
) is well-posed even in Sobolev spaces
of very low regularities.

The next definition concerns a particular notion of irregularity of the modulation
w
,
introduced in
[
19
,
28
]
,
which will play a fundamental role in our analysis.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\rho>0
and
0<\gamma<1
.
Given
T>0
, we say that a function
w\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R})
is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular
on the time interval
[0,T]
if we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}:=\sup_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{0\leq r<t\leq T}\langle a\rangle^{\rho}\frac{\left|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(a)\right|}{|t-r|^{\gamma}}<\infty,

(1.5)

where

\displaystyle\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(a)=\int_{r}^{t}e^{iaw(t^{\prime})}dt^{\prime}.

(1.6)

We say that
w
is

(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if it is

(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular
on
[0,T]
for each finite
T>0
.

For further study on the notion of irregularity,
we refer readers to
recent works
[
59
,
126
]

which appeared after the first version of the current paper.

In order to study (
1.4
) with an irregular modulation
w
,
we need to exploit the temporal regularity of a solution
u
.
More precisely,
we will exploit the temporal regularity
of the

modulated interaction representation

\mathbf{u}
of the unknown
u
defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}(t)=U^{w}(t)^{-1}u(t).

(1.7)

When
w(t)=t

(i.e. for usual (unmodulated) dispersive PDEs), the (standard) interaction representation
e^{-tL}u(t)

has played a fundamental role
in the Fourier restriction norm method

[
6
,
87
]

(see also
[
63
]
,
where an analogy
to the
interaction representation in quantum mechanics was made)
and the normal form method
in both the deterministic and probabilistic settings

[
3
,
89
,
72
,
117
,
116
,
119
]
.
In general, there are two advantages
in working with (modulated) interaction representations:
(i) it allows us to exploit temporal regularity of
the interaction representation,
while, at the level of the original unknown, positive temporal regularity normally comes
with a loss of spatial derivative
(precisely due to
the presence of
the modulated linear propagator

U^{w}(t)

in (
1.4
))
and
(ii) it allows us to show multilinear dispersive smoothing
in a more direct manner.

In terms of the modulated interaction representation
\mathbf{u}
,
the Duhamel formulation (
1.4
)
is written as

\mathbf{u}(t)=u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{N}(U^{w}(t^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(t^{\prime}))dt^{\prime}.

(1.8)

In this paper, we study the fixed point problem (
1.8
)
by assuming that a certain regularity condition
on
the modulated interaction representation
\mathbf{u}
.
A particular subspace of
C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
will play a special role in our analysis,
which we introduce in the next definition.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{T}
,

s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Suppose that

w\in C(I;\mathbb{R})

for an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}

is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular for some
\rho>0
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
.
Then, we
denote by

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{w}^{s}(I\times\mathcal{M})\subset C(I;H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

the space of
paths controlled by
w
,
which are
by paths
u\in C(I;H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

such that
their modulated interaction representations

\mathbf{u}(t)=U^{w}(t)^{-1}u(t)

belong
to
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(I;H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
; see (
2.3
)
for the definition of the
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
-norm.

We also define the class

\dot{\mathscr{D}}_{w}^{s}(I\times\mathcal{M})

in a similar manner.
Namely,
we say that

u\in C(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

belongs to

\dot{\mathscr{D}}_{w}^{s}(I\times\mathcal{M})

if its modulated interaction representation

\mathbf{u}

belongs
to
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(I;\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.

We conclude this subsection by presenting an example
of irregular modulations.
In
[
19
]
,
the authors proved that a fractional Brownian motion is a
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular function.

Theorem A
.

Let
\{W_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
be a fractional Brownian motion
of Hurst index
H\in(0,1)
.
Then,
for any
\rho<\frac{1}{2H}
,
there exists
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1

such that, with probability one, the sample paths of

W
are
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Theorem
A

shows that there exist continuous paths which are
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular for arbitrarily large
\rho
.
In a later work
[
59
]
, it was shown that
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregularity is a generic property of Hölder functions of sufficiently low regularity; see
[
59
, p. 4 and Theorem 32]
.

Theorem B
.

Let
d\geq 1
.
Given any
\delta\in(0,1)
, almost every
\delta
-Hölder continuous function
w\in C^{\delta}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})

is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular for any
\rho<\frac{1}{2\delta}
with some
\gamma=\gamma(\rho)\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

In this statement, “almost every” is to be understood according to the notion of
prevalence
; see
[
59
]
for details and the references therein.

As observed in
[
19
,
28
]
, the irregularity of the path
w
allows
us to obtain a regularizing effect in the ODE context and
to prove pathwise well-posedness for
the modulated NLS.
In the present paper, we show that irregular modulations
induce strong regularization effects
in the case of KdV
and related equations.
In order to treat
irregular modulations in the sense of Definition
1.1
,
we will use a nonlinear version of Young integration theory introduced in
[
28
]

to give a proper meaning to the integral term in (
1.8
);
see Section
3
,
where we provide an extensive review on the nonlinear Young integration theory
for non-experts.
These ideas, essentially coming from the controlled path theory
[
64
]
, were already used in the context of the (unmodulated) KdV (
1.3
) on the circle in the work
[
65
]

by the second author,
where the equation has been studied in Sobolev spaces (and more general Fourier–Lebesgue spaces)
of negative regularity,
without relying on the Fourier restriction norm method.
We also point out that the refinement of the Fourier restriction norm method
due to Koch and Tataru
[
87
]

is also based on interpreting the Duhamel integral (at the level of the interaction representation)
as an ‘endpoint Young integral’ via the
U^{2}
-
V^{2}
duality.
We refer interested readers to the lecture notes
[
86
]
;
see also
[
73
]
.
Lastly, we mention recent works
[
26
,
25
]

on
pathwise well-posedness of stochastic dispersive PDEs
with multiplicative noises,
where
the Young integration theory and
the controlled path approach
are combined with
the Fourier restriction norm method.

1.3.
Well-posedness results

In this subsection,
we state our main results on well-posedness
of the modulated dispersive equations mentioned above.
For simplicity of the presentation,
we impose
the mean-zero assumption
3
3

3

For the modulated KdV, BO, and ILW equations,
if initial data has non-zero mean
\alpha_{0}
, then the following Galilean transformation:

u(t,x)\mapsto u(t,x+2\alpha_{0}t)-\alpha_{0}
,
along with the conservation of the spatial mean (which can be verified
by arguing as in Section
6
),
transforms
these equations with a non-zero mean into the mean-zero versions.
Note that, while the mean-zero assumption is not necessary
for the modulated mKdV (
1.13
),
we need to apply a gauge transform and consider its renormalized version (
1.14
).

on solutions to
these modulated dispersive equations on the circle
in the remaining part of this paper.

Our primary model example is the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on both the circle and the real line.
Before stating our main result on the modulated KdV (
1.2
),
let us go over the known well-posedness (and ill-posedness) results
on the (unmodulated) KdV (
1.3
).
KdV has attracted wide attention
from both the applied and theoretical communities
and its well-posedness has been studied
extensively on both the circle and the real line.
In
[
6
]
,
Bourgain introduced the so-called
Fourier restriction norm method
and proved local and global well-posedness of (
1.3
)
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{R}
,
which was
improved
by the subsequent works

[
80
,
31
,
84
,
71
]

(see also
[
12
]
)
and now KdV (
1.3
)
is known to be
globally well-posed
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s\geq-\frac{1}{2}

and in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for
s\geq-\frac{3}{4}
.
In particular, in these works,
local well-posedness was shown by a contraction argument,
exhibiting semilinear nature of the equation.
In the following, we refer local well-posedness
by a contraction argument
as
semilinear local well-posedness
.
On the other hand, it is known that the solution map
fails to be locally
uniformly continuous below the aforementioned regularity thresholds

[
29
]
; see also
[
9
]
.
Namely, the KdV equation (
1.3
) exhibits

quasilinear
nature in the low regularity setting
(i.e. below
s=-\frac{1}{2}
on
\mathbb{T}

and
s=-\frac{3}{4}
on
\mathbb{R}
).
Compare the situation with the (unmodulated) BO and ILW equations,
which are known to behave quasilinearly
for any regularity; see Remark
1.12
.

There are also well-posedness results of KdV based on its complete integrability;
see

[
78
,
83
]

for the sharp global well-posedness of KdV in
H^{-1}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{R}

(see
[
99
,
100
]

for ill-posedness below
s=-1
).
We note that
the time modulation effect
breaks the integrability of the KdV equation,
and thus we aim to develop a Fourier analytic approach
adapted to the current modulated setting.

We now state our main well-posedness
results
for the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on the circle.

Theorem 1.3
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy one of the following conditions
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(i.a)}&\ \ \tfrac{1}{2}\leq\rho\leq\tfrac{3}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad s>\tfrac{3}{2}-3\rho,\\
\textup{(i.b)}&\ \ \rho>\tfrac{3}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad s\geq-\rho.\end{split}

(1.9)

Then, the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{T}

is semilinearly locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

(ii)

In addition, suppose that

w
is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

and that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy one of the following conditions
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(ii.a)}&\ \ \tfrac{1}{2}<\rho\leq\tfrac{3}{4\gamma}\quad\text{and}\quad s>\tfrac{3-6\rho}{6-4\gamma},\\
\textup{(ii.b)}&\ \ \tfrac{3}{4\gamma}<\rho<\tfrac{3}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad s\geq-\rho,\\
\textup{(ii.c)}&\ \ \rho\geq\tfrac{3}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad s>-\tfrac{3}{2}.\end{split}

(1.10)

Then,
the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{T}

is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

Remark 1.4
.

(i)
Uniqueness of a solution
u
constructed in Theorem
1.3
(i)
holds
in the class
\mathscr{D}_{w,T}^{s}([0,\tau]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in Definition
1.2
,
where

0<\tau\leq T
denotes the local existence time.
A similar comment applies to the other modulated equations
considered in this paper.

(ii)
The solution map:

(u_{0},w)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\times\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}\longmapsto\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

is locally Lipschitz continuous;
see Remarks
3.9
(iii) and
4.2
.
A similar comment applies to the other modulated equations
considered in this paper.

(iii) All of our local well-posedness results
in this paper
come with
persistence of regularity
.
Suppose that a modulated equation is locally well-posed
in
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
for some
s\in\mathbb{R}

with local existence time
4
4

4

Clearly,
\tau
also depends
on the modulation
w
but we suppress its dependence on
w

in this discussion.

\tau=\tau(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}})\in(0,T]
.
If, in addition, we have
u_{0}\in H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})

for some
s_{0}>s
,
then the corresponding solution exists
with the same local existence time
\tau
,
depending only on the
H^{s}
-norm of the initial data
u_{0}
.

As a corollary to Theorem
1.3
(i)
with Theorem
A
, we have
the following well-posedness
result for the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on
\mathbb{T}
,
where a modulation is given by a fractional Brownian motion.
For simplicity of the presentation,
we only state its local well-posedness.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
\{W_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
be a fractional Brownian motion
of Hurst index
H\in(0,1)
.
Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies

(a)

\displaystyle\ \ \tfrac{2}{3}\leq H<1\quad\text{and}\quad s>\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{3}{2H},

(b)

\displaystyle\ \ 0<H<\tfrac{2}{3}\quad\text{and}\quad s>-\tfrac{1}{2H}.

Then,
with probability one,
the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with the modulation given by
a sample path of the
fractional Brownian motion

\{W_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}

is semilinearly locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

Remark 1.6
.

Similar results hold for each of the dispersive equations considered in this paper, with the appropriate choice of exponents, when the modulation function is given by a fractional Brownian motion, or, thanks to Theorem
B
, when the modulation function is given by a generic (in the sense of prevalence) Hölder function.

Next,
we state our main well-posedness
results
for the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on the real line.

Theorem 1.7
.

Given
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy one of the following conditions
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(i.a)}&\ \ \tfrac{1}{2}<\rho\leq\tfrac{3}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad s>\tfrac{3}{2}-3\rho,\\
\textup{(i.b)}&\ \ \tfrac{3}{4}<\rho<\tfrac{3}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad s\geq-\rho,\\
\textup{(i.c)}&\ \ \rho\geq\tfrac{3}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad s>-\tfrac{3}{2}.\end{split}

(1.11)

Then, the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{R}

is semilinearly locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
.

(ii)

In addition, suppose that

w
is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

and that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy (
1.10
).
Then,
the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{R}

is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
.

Theorems
1.3
and
1.7

show
that an irregular modulation provides a
regularization effect
on the KdV equation,
allowing us to prove well-posedness in the regime, where the unmodulated
KdV equation (
1.3
) is ill-posed
(namely,
s<-1
).
We also mention that our construction of a solution is based on a contraction argument,
yielding local Lipschitz continuous dependence.
Such a semilinear property fails for
the unmodulated
KdV equation (
1.3
)
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s<-\frac{1}{2}

and in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for
s<-\frac{3}{4}
,
thus exhibiting a
semilinearization phenomenon
.
See Theorem
1.11

and Remark
1.12

below for an even stronger
semilinearization phenomenon on the modulated BO and ILW equations.
Last but not least, in the periodic case,
Theorem
1.3
shows
a very strong regularization effect;
given
any

s\in\mathbb{R}
,
the modulated KdV (
1.2
)
on the circle
with a sufficiently irregular modulation
is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
; see also Theorem
1.11

for an analogous result on the modulated BO and ILW equations.
Theorems
1.3
and
1.7

present the first
results
on regularization by noise
in
the study of nonlinear dispersive equations with rough (deterministic) initial data.
See also Remark
1.8
.
In the next subsection,
we also present another kind of regularization-by-noise phenomenon
in the context of

nonlinear smoothing
;
see Theorem
1.14
.

Let us point out that our results are limited to
sufficiently irregular modulations
and they do not provide any sharp information about the dependence of solutions on modulations, especially about the transition between smooth and irregular modulations.
Indeed, a bit surprisingly, in the current modulated context, the application of
controlled path techniques (including nonlinear Young integration theory via the sewing lemma) is easier if the modulation is very irregular.
In fact, by considering
sufficiently irregular modulations,
we have avoided the need of
a second-order controlled expansion which was necessary in
the work
[
65
]
, where the second author developed a controlled path
approach to study the (unmodulated) KdV equation (
1.3
).
It remains an open problem to fill the gap between regular and irregular modulations.

Our main strategy for proving local well-posedness is to make sense of the integral term in (
1.8
)
as a
nonlinear Young integral

and prove local well-posedness of the equation (
1.8
)
via a contraction argument,
just as in the previous work
[
28
]

by the first two authors.
While our approach is equivalent
to that presented in
[
28
]
,
we first construct the integral in (
1.8
)
as a nonlinear Young integral

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})
with a given driver
X

in an abstract setting
via the sewing lemma
(Lemma
3.3
)
from
the theory of controlled rough paths
[
64
]

developed by the second author; see Lemma
3.4
.
We made a particular effort to
present this part
in a pedestrian manner, accessible
to non-experts.
We then study well-posedness and related properties of the following
Young differential equation (YDE):

\mathbf{u}=u_{0}+\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})

(1.12)

in our functional framework adapted to Sobolev spaces,
under appropriate assumptions
on the driver
X
;
see Proposition
3.8
.
In Sections
4
and
5
,
we verify
that such assumptions are indeed satisfied
for each of the modulated dispersive equations
we consider in this paper.

The controlled rough path theory,
introduced
as an alternative formulation of the rough path theory
due to Lyons

[
95
,
96
]
,
has been applied and extended further in a variety of contexts;
for example,

•

well-posedness of stochastic parabolic equations
[
70
,
48
]
,

•

Hairer’s work on well-posedness for the KPZ equation
[
74
]
,

•

Hairer’s theory of regularity structures,
dealing with very singular parabolic PDEs such as the parabolic

\Phi_{3}^{4}
-model
[
75
]
;

•

paracontrolled calculus
[
67
,
18
,
103
]

which has also been applied
to study nonlinear wave equations (NLW)
in recent years

[
68
,
110
,
10
,
111
,
11
]
.

Our approach to study modulated dispersive equations,
exhibiting various regularization-by-noise effects
is an interesting and useful addition to this list.

All our techniques are deterministic and they provide novel results even in the stochastic context; for example, we can take the modulation
w
to be the sample path of a fractional Brownian motion
as we have seen in Corollary
1.5
.
Even for a Brownian motion, our results on KdV, mKdV, BO, and ILW are novel to our knowledge.
See Subsections
1.5
and
1.6

for applications with random initial data and stochastic forcing.
We also note that
YDEs
allow for a straightforward Euler approximation scheme
to discretize in time; see, for example,
[
28
, Subsection 2.3]
.

In Section
7
, we prove the global well-posedness results
in Theorems
1.3
(ii) and
1.7
(ii)
by the so-called
I
-method (= method of almost conservation laws)
introduced in
[
31
]
.
In particular, we prove these results
by
combining a key crucial commutator estimate (Proposition
7.6
),
a common tool in an application
of the
I
-method, with the sewing lemma (Lemma
3.3
).
Such a combination of tools and ideas from dispersive PDEs and the controlled rough path theory is novel in the literature.
We also mention a recent work
[
69
]

for
a highly non-trivial application of
the
I
-method in the context of the stochastic NLW.
The
I
-method relies on the scaling property
of a given equation and thus is limited to the scaling-subcritical regime

s>s_{\text{crit}}=-\frac{3}{2}
in the KdV case.
This is the reason why the restriction
s>-\frac{3}{2}
appears in Theorems
1.3
(ii)
and
1.7
(ii).
On the other, it is
intriguing to see the same scaling-subcritical restriction appears
in local well-posedness on the real line (Theorem
1.7
(i)).
Our implementation of the
I
-method in the current modulated context is of the first order.
It would be of interest to see if we can improve the range of regularity
by adding a “correction” term
and proceed with the second order
I
-method;
see, for example,
[
31
]
for a higher order implementation of the
I
-method
in the unmodulated setting.

Remark 1.8
.

In
[
7
,
8
]
, Bourgain initiated the study
of nonlinear dispersive equations with random initial data of low regularity.
Over the last fifteen years,
this study has been particularly active

[
8
,
15
,
34
,
16
,
4
,
120
,
112
]
;
see
[
5
]
for a (slightly outdated) survey on this topic.
See also more recent works

[
68
,
47
,
110
,
10
,
111
,
11
]
, including those
on stochastic dispersive PDEs with additive noises
(which can be easily adapted to random initial data).
In these works,
non-deterministic viewpoints
allowed us to go beyond the limit of deterministic analysis,
and thus,
broadly speaking,
we can regard them as
regularization-by-noise phenomena
(by considering random initial data).

We also note that
a noise can sometimes worsen the behavior of an equation;
see,
for example,

[
39
,
40
]

for the construction of
finite-time blowup for the stochastic NLS.

Let us now consider other modulated dispersive equations.
For simplicity of presentation,
we restrict our attention to the periodic case.
We first consider the following
modulated
modified KdV equation (mKdV) on
\mathbb{T}
:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{3}.

(1.13)

As in the unmodulated setting
[
6
]
,
the equation (
1.13
) on the circle is not suitable
for well-posedness studies.
Using the
L^{2}
-conservation,
5
5

5

In the current modulated setting,
we need to justify the
L^{2}
-conservation; see Section
6
.

the gauge transform:
x\mapsto x-\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}t

converts (
1.13
)
into the following renormalized mKdV:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=3\big(u^{2}-\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)\partial_{x}u,

(1.14)

where certain resonant interactions have been removed;
see (
4.16
) and (
4.17
).

Theorem 1.9
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
Then,
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
the modulated
(
renormalized
)
mKdV equation (
1.14
)
on
\mathbb{T}

is semilinearly locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

At the level of the original modulated mKdV (
1.13
),
local well-posedness holds
in a class
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\cap\{\|f\|_{L^{2}}=\mu\}

for any prescribed value of
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Remark 1.10
.

In the unmodulated setting,
mKdV on the circle is known to be semilinearly locally well-posed in

H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s\geq\frac{1}{2}

[
6
,
89
]
,
while
the solution map
fails to be locally
uniformly continuous below
s=\frac{1}{2}

[
29
,
129
]
; see also
[
9
]
.
See
[
79
,
54
]

for well-posedness results via the complete integrability of the equation.

Unlike the KdV case,
Theorem
1.9

does not provide any improvement as compared to the unmodulated setting.
This is due to the fact that
the regularity restriction
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
in Theorem
1.9

comes from the resonant part of the nonlinearity,
where the modulation plays no role;
see the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

We note out that
Theorem
1.9

also applied to the following
complex-valued modulated mKdV on the circle:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=3\big(|u|^{2}-\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)\partial_{x}u,

(1.15)

yielding its semilinear local well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
On the other hand,
it is easy to see that a function

u_{N}(t,x)=a_{N}e^{i(Nx+N^{3}w(t)-3i|a_{N}|^{2}t)}

is a solution to (
1.15
) for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{N}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then,
by following the argument in
[
13
]

(see also
[
117
, Lemma 6.16]
6
6

6

Lemma A.6 in the arXiv version.
),
one can easily show that semilinear local well-posedness
of (
1.15
)
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

fails for
s<\frac{1}{2}
.
By adapting the approach in
[
129
,
106
]

(see also
[
118
,
44
]
)
to weaken the resonant interaction,
one may be able to improve Theorem
1.9
for some
s<\frac{1}{2}
,
but we do not purse this issue here.
See also Remark
1.16
.
We also mention the recent works

[
20
,
21
]

for a further discussion on the renormalization of the complex-valued mKdV.

Next, we consider the following
modulated Benjamin-Ono equation (BO) on the circle:

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{2},

(1.16)

where
\mathcal{H}
denotes the
Hilbert transform
with the Fourier multiplier
-\mathbf{1}_{n\neq 0}\cdot i\sgn(n)
.
Given
0<\delta<\infty
, we also consider the
modulated
intermediate long wave equation (ILW) on the circle:

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{2},

(1.17)

where the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
is given
as the following Fourier multiplier operator:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}f}(n)=\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}(n)\widehat{f}(n):=-\mathbf{1}_{n\neq 0}\cdot i\bigg(\coth(\delta n)-\frac{1}{\delta n}\bigg)\widehat{f}(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(1.18)

In the unmodulated setting (i.e.
w(t)=t
),
the ILW equation
models the propagation of an internal wave at the interface of a stratified fluid of
finite depth
\delta>0
,
and the unknown
u
denotes the amplitude of the internal wave at the interface.
Moreover, it serves as an “intermediate” equation of finite depth
0<\delta<\infty
,
providing a natural connection
between the BO equation, modeling fluid of infinite depth (
\delta=\infty
),
and the KdV equation (
1.3
), modeling shallow water (
\delta=0
).
See
[
127
,
85
]
for an overview of the subject and the references therein.

In considering
the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
),
we need to magnify the amplitude
u

of the internal wave at the interface
to observe any non-trivial limiting behavior.
For this purpose, we introduce the following scaling transform
[
1
]
:

\displaystyle v(t,x)=\tfrac{3}{\delta}u\big(\tfrac{3}{\delta}t,x\big),

(1.19)

which converts (
1.17
)
into the following modulated scaled ILW equation:

\partial_{t}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}v\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}v^{2},

(1.20)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=\frac{3}{\delta}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}.

(1.21)

We first state well-posedness results
of these modulated equations.

Theorem 1.11
.

Given
\rho\geq 1
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
\rho\geq 1
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy one of the following conditions
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(i.a)}&\ \ \rho=1\quad\text{and}\quad s>-\tfrac{1}{2},\\
\textup{(i.b)}&\ \ \rho>1\quad\text{and}\quad s\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho.\end{split}

(1.22)

Then, the modulated BO equation (
1.16
)
on
\mathbb{T}

is semilinearly locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.
Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
the modulated
ILW (
1.17
)
and
the modulated
scaled ILW (
1.20
)
on
\mathbb{T}

are also semilinearly locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

(ii)

In addition, suppose that

w
is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then, the modulated BO equation (
1.16
)
on
\mathbb{T}
is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s\geq 0
.
Similarly,
given
0<\delta<\infty
,
the modulated
ILW (
1.17
)
and
the modulated
scaled ILW (
1.20
)
on
\mathbb{T}
are globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s\geq 0
.

In the unmodulated setting,
Fourier analytic approaches
allow us to show well-posedness of
BO and the (scaled) ILW in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

[
97
,
98
,
101
,
23
]
;
see also
[
46
]
.
Furthermore,
by the complete integrability of the equation,
the (unmodulated) BO equation
is known to be globally well-posed
in the entire scaling-subcritical regime,
namely in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s>-\frac{1}{2}
,
while it is ill-posed for
s\leq-\frac{1}{2}

[
60
,
81
]
.
See also
[
22
]

for the study of ILW in negative Sobolev spaces.
In the current modulated setting,
Theorem
1.11

shows that,
given any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
the modulated BO, ILW, and scaled ILW
on the circle
with sufficiently irregular modulations
are locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
exhibiting a strong regularization-by-noise phenomenon.

Remark 1.12
.

In the unmodulated setting,
BO and ILW
are known to behave
quasilinearly
and, in particular,
semilinear local well-posedness
on
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

fails for
any

s\in\mathbb{R}

[
102
,
61
]
.
See also
[
88
]
for a related result on the real line.
Theorem
1.11

shows that,
given any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
irregular modulations
semilinearize these equations,
exhibiting
a strong regularization-by-noise
phenomenon
in the form of semilinearization.

Lastly, we consider deep-water convergence (
\delta\to\infty
)
and shallow-water convergence (
\delta\to 0
)
of the modulated (scaled) ILW.
In the unmodulated setting,
the convergence issue
for the (scaled) ILW
has been studied in

[
1
,
92
,
93
,
23
,
55
,
24
]

from both deterministic and probabilistic viewpoints.
The following theorem establishes
deep-water
and shallow-water convergence results
for the modulated (scaled) ILW.

Theorem 1.13
.

Given
\rho>1
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i) (deep-water convergence).

Let

s>-\frac{1}{2}\rho
.
Given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
u^{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
, and
u
denote the local-in-time solutions
to the modulated ILW equation (
1.17
)
and the modulated BO equation (
1.16
)
with
u^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
, respectively.
Then, as
\delta\to\infty
,

u^{\delta}
converges to
u
in

C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated BO equation
(
1.16
).
Moreover, if

w
is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

and

s\geq 0
,
then the deep-water convergence holds globally in time.

(ii) (shallow-water convergence).

Let

s>-\frac{1}{2}\rho
.
Given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
v^{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
, and
u
denote the local-in-time solutions
to the modulated scaled ILW equation (
1.20
)
and the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
with
v^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
, respectively.
Then, as
\delta\to 0
,

v^{\delta}
converges to
u
in

C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated KdV equation
(
1.2
).
Moreover, if

w
is
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

and

s\geq 0
,
then the shallow-water convergence holds globally in time.

It follows from
the discussion in
Subsections
5.3

and
5.4

together with
Lemma
3.1
(iv),
Remark
3.2
,
and
Proposition
3.8
(iii)
that, at the level of the modulated interaction representations,
convergence holds
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.
For example,

\mathbf{u}^{\delta}(t)=e^{-w(t)\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}}u^{\delta}(t)

converges
to
\mathbf{u}(t)=e^{-w(t)\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}}u(t)

in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

as
\delta\to\infty
.

1.4.
Nonlinear smoothing & Galerkin approximation

Our approach to modulated dispersive equations on the circle
also yields
(i) nonlinear smoothing
and (ii) convergence of the Galerkin approximation.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we consider the
the following truncated modulated KdV on the circle:

\partial_{t}u^{N}+\partial_{x}^{3}u^{N}\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}u^{N})^{2}\big),

(1.23)

where
\mathbf{P}_{N}
denotes the Dirichlet projector onto the (spatial) frequencies

\{|n|\leq N\}
; see (
2.1
).
Then, a slight modification of the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i)
yields the following results
for the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on the circle.

Theorem 1.14
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i) (nonlinear smoothing).

Suppose that
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.9
).
In addition,
suppose that
s_{0}>s
satisfies
one of the following conditions
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(i.a)}&\ \ 0\leq s+\rho\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{0}<2s+3\rho-\tfrac{3}{2},\\
\textup{(i.b)}&\ \ s+\rho>\tfrac{1}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{0}\leq s+2\rho-1.\end{split}

(1.24)

Given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
u\in C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
be the solution
to the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
,
where

\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time.
Then, we have

\displaystyle u-e^{-w(t)\partial_{x}^{3}}u_{0}\in C([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T})).

(ii) (Galerkin approximation).

Suppose that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy one of the following conditions
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(ii.a)}&\ \ \tfrac{1}{2}<\rho\leq\tfrac{3}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad s>\tfrac{3}{2}-3\rho,\\
\textup{(ii.b)}&\ \ \rho>\tfrac{3}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad s>-\rho.\end{split}

(1.25)

Then, given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
the solution
u^{N}
to the truncated modulated KdV equation (
1.23
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with
u^{N}|_{t=0}=u_{0}

converges to the solution
u

to the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{T}
with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

in
C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated KdV equation
(
1.2
).
Moreover,
given any
r>0
,
the rate of convergence of
u^{N}
to
u
is
uniform in
u_{0}\in B_{r}

where

B_{r}
denotes the ball
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

of radius
r>0
centered at the origin.

We point out that
our argument does not yield
nonlinear smoothing or convergence
of the Galerkin approximation
for the modulated KdV on the real line;
see Remark
4.5
.

In the case of the (unmodulated) KdV (
1.3
) on
\mathbb{T}
,
nonlinear smoothing
was shown
for
s>-\frac{1}{2}
and
s_{0}<\min(3s+1,s+1)

by the normal form method

[
50
]
.
We prove Theorem
1.14
(i)
by a slight modification of the contraction argument
needed to prove local well-posedness
and thus we do not need to rely on
the normal form method.
Furthermore,
the regularity gain on the nonlinear part

u-e^{-w(t)\partial_{x}^{3}}u_{0}

can be (arbitrarily) large
for irregular modulations,
which is in sharp contrast to the unmodulated case.
We also point out that
the uniformity claim in
Theorem
1.14
(ii)
fails for
the (unmodulated) KdV (
1.3
) on
\mathbb{T}

(see
[
33
, Theorem 1.1]
),
which shows another example of regularization by noise
for the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on the circle.

Next, we state analogous results for the modulated mKdV (
1.13
) on the circle.

Theorem 1.15
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i) (nonlinear smoothing).

Suppose that

s_{0}>s\geq\frac{1}{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle s_{0}\leq\min(s+2\min(\rho,s)-1,3s-1).

(1.26)

Given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
u\in C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
be the solution
to the modulated mKdV equation (
1.13
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
,
where

\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time.
Then, we have

\displaystyle u-e^{-w(t)\partial_{x}^{3}}u_{0}\in C([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T})).

(ii) (Galerkin approximation).

Let

s>\frac{1}{2}
.
Then, given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
the solution
u^{N}
to the truncated modulated mKdV equation
on
\mathbb{T}
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u^{N}+\partial_{x}^{3}u^{N}\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}u^{N})^{3}\big)\\
u^{N}|_{t=0}=u_{0}\end{cases}

converges to the solution
u

to the modulated mKdV equation (
1.13
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

in
C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated mKdV equation
(
1.13
).
Moreover,
given any
r>0
,
the rate of convergence of
u^{N}
to
u
is
uniform in
u_{0}\in B_{r}\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

Remark 1.16
.

Contrary to Theorem
1.14
(i) for
the modulated KdV (
1.2
) (and also Theorem
1.17
(i) for
the modulated BO (
1.16
)),
the gain of regularity
in nonlinear smoothing for the modulated mKdV (
1.13
)
is bounded by
3s-1
even if we take
\rho\gg 1
.
This is (once again) due to the resonant part of the nonlinearity, where
the modulation plays no role;
see the proof of Proposition
4.3
.
See also Remark
1.10
.

Lastly, we state the corresponding results
for the modulated BO and the modulated (scaled) ILW
on the circle.

Theorem 1.17
.

Given
\rho>1
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i) (nonlinear smoothing).

Suppose that
\rho>1
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy (
1.22
).
In addition, suppose that
s_{0}>s
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{(i.a)}&\ \ 0\leq s<s_{0}\leq\rho-1,\\
\textup{(i.b)}&\ \ s<0\quad\text{and}\quad s_{0}\leq s+\rho-1.\end{split}

(1.27)

Given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
u\in C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
be the solution
to the modulated BO equation (
1.16
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
,
where

\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time.
Then, we have

\displaystyle u-e^{w(t)\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}\in C([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T})).

(ii) (Galerkin approximation).

Suppose that

\rho>1

and
s>-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho
.
Then, given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
the solution
u^{N}
to the truncated modulated BO equation
on
\mathbb{T}
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u^{N}-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u^{N}\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}u^{N})^{2}\big)\\
u^{N}|_{t=0}=u_{0}\end{cases}

converges to the solution
u

to the modulated BO equation (
1.16
) on
\mathbb{T}

with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

in
C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated BO equation (
1.16
).
Moreover,
given any
r>0
,
the rate of convergence of
u^{N}
to
u
is
uniform in
u_{0}\in B_{r}\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

(iii)

Let
0<\delta<\infty
.
Then,
the conclusions in Part (i) and (ii)
also hold
for the modulated
ILW equation (
1.17
)
and
the modulated
scaled ILW equation (
1.20
)
on
\mathbb{T}
.

1.5.
Invariance of the white noise under the modulated KdV

In Theorem
1.3
(ii),
we established global well-posedness
of the periodic modulated KdV (
1.2
)
via a deterministic tool (the
I
-method).
In this subsection,
we use a probabilistic tool
to globalize solutions to
the periodic modulated KdV (
1.2
),
which allows us to construct global-in-time solutions
in a regime not covered by
Theorem
1.3
(ii).

Let
\mu
be
a white noise
7
7

7

As it is customary in the literature,
with a slight abuse of notation,
we use the term ‘white noise’ to refer to both the distribution-valued random variable
u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
)
and its law
\mu=\Law(u_{0}^{\omega})
, when there is no confusion.
Here,
\Law(X)
denotes the law of a random variable
X
.
For clarity, we may refer to
\mu=\Law(u_{0}^{\omega})
as the white noise measure.

on the circle
\mathbb{T}
with formal density:

\displaystyle d\mu=Z^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{2}dx_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg)du.

(1.28)

More precisely,
\mu
is the induced probability
measure
under the map:
8
8

8

For simplicity of
the presentation, we assume that the white noise has spatial mean zero on
\mathbb{T}
.

\displaystyle\omega\in\Omega\longmapsto u_{0}^{\omega}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}g_{n}(\omega)e_{n},

(1.29)

where
\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,

e_{n}(x)=e^{inx}
, and

\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
is a family of independent standard
complex-valued Gaussian random variables
9
9

9

Note that
\text{Var}(g_{n})=2
.

on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})

conditioned that
g_{-n}=\overline{g_{n}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
It is easy to see that

u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
) belongs almost surely to
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\setminus H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
,
s<-\frac{1}{2}
.

In particular,
Theorem
1.3
(i)
states that, for
\rho>\frac{2}{3}
,
the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on
\mathbb{T}

is locally well-posed almost surely with respect to the white noise initial data.
On the other hand,
the regularity threshold
in (
1.10
) for (deterministic)
global well-posedness tends to
-\frac{1}{4}

as
\rho\to\frac{2}{3}
and
\gamma\to\frac{1}{2}
.
More precisely,
when
\rho<1-\frac{1}{3}\gamma
,
the regularity threshold in (
1.10
) is above
-\frac{1}{2}

and thus
we can not apply Theorem
1.3
(ii)
to globalize local-in-time solutions with the white noise initial data.

By using a probabilistic argument,
we establish the following global-in-time result
for
the periodic modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
with the white noise initial data,
which complements the deterministic
global well-posedness result in Theorem
1.3
,(i).

Theorem 1.18
.

Given
\rho>\frac{2}{3}
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
Then, the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
on the circle
\mathbb{T}

is almost surely globally well-posed
with the white noise initial data
10
10

10

As it is common
in the study of dispersive PDEs with random initial data,
we use
u_{0}^{\omega}
to denote both the white noise
and a sample path of the white noise but the meaning is clear from the context.

u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
),
and the white noise
\mu
is invariant under the resulting dynamics.

Theorem
1.18

extends
invariance of the white noise under the KdV dynamics
from the unmodulated setting (see
[
121
,
107
,
108
,
109
,
115
,
82
]
)
to the current modulated setting,
thus establishing the first result
on the construction of invariant measures
for modulated dispersive PDEs.
We also mention
that
Theorem
1.18

plays a key role in the globalization
argument for
the stochastic modulated KdV (
1.30
)
with an additive space-time white noise
(Theorem
1.21
(ii))
considered in the next subsection.

In view of
Theorem
1.14
on
the convergence of the truncated modulated KdV dynamics (
1.23
)
to the modulated KdV dynamics (
1.2
),
Theorem
1.18

follows from a standard application of
Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
7
,
8
]

(whose details we omit; see
[
14
]
for
details of Bourgain’s invariant measure argument),
once we prove invariance of the white noise
for the truncated modulated KdV (
1.23
); see Proposition
8.1
.

Theorem
1.11

states that
for
\rho>1
,
the modulated BO (
1.16
),
the modulated ILW (
1.17
),
and the modulated scaled ILW (
1.20
)
are locally well-posed
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
s>-\frac{1}{2}\rho
,
containing the support of the white noise.
Moreover,
for the same range of
\rho
and
s
,
Theorem
1.17
(ii)
guarantees convergence of the Galerkin approximation.
Hence,
a slight modification of a proof of Theorem
1.18

yields
the following result
on almost sure global well-posedness
of these equations with the white noise initial data
and invariance of the white noise.
We omit details.

Theorem 1.19
.

Given
\rho>1
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

The modulated BO equation (
1.16
)
on the circle
\mathbb{T}

is almost surely globally well-posed
with the white noise initial data

u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
),
and the white noise
\mu
is invariant under the resulting dynamics.
The same result also
holds
for the modulated ILW equation (
1.17
)
and the modulated scaled ILW equation (
1.20
) on the circle.

(ii)

Let
\{\delta_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence of positive numbers, tending to
\infty

as
j\to\infty
.
Let
u^{\delta_{j}}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
u
denote the solutions
to the modulated ILW equation (
1.17
)
and the modulated BO equation (
1.16
)
with the white noise initial data

u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
), respectively.
Then, as
j\to\infty

(
and hence
\delta_{j}\to\infty
)
,

u^{\delta_{j}}
converges to
u
in

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\varepsilon>0
,
almost surely.
Here,

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

is endowed with the compact-open topology.

(iii)

Let
\{\delta_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a decreasing sequence of positive numbers, tending to
0

as
j\to\infty
.
Let
v^{\delta_{j}}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
u
denote the solutions
to the modulated scaled ILW equation (
1.20
)
and the modulated KdV equation (
1.2
)
with the white noise initial data

u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
), respectively.
Then, as
j\to\infty

(
and hence
\delta_{j}\to 0
)
,

v^{\delta_{j}}
converges to
u
in

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\varepsilon>0
,
almost surely.

Remark 1.20
.

In Theorem
1.19
(ii),
we took a discrete sequence of depth parameters
\delta_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
such that, in view of
Theorem
1.19
(i),
there exists a set of full probability,
guaranteeing global existence of

u^{\delta_{j}}
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

In the case of a continuous parameter
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
we have almost sure asymptotic global-in-time deep-water convergence
in the following sense.
It follows from Theorem
1.19
(i)
that there exists
\Sigma\subset\Omega
with
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma)=1

such that for each
\omega\in\Sigma
,
the solution
u

to the modulated BO (
1.16
)
with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}^{\omega}

exists globally in time.
Given any
T\gg 1
, by iterating the local-in-time argument
(as in the proof of Theorem
1.13
(i)),
we see that there exists
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\omega,T)\gg 1

such that, for any
\delta>\delta_{0}
,
the solution
u^{\delta}

to the modulated ILW (
1.17
)
with
u^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u_{0}^{\omega}

exists on the time interval
[0,T]

and,
u^{\delta}
moreover converges to
u
on
[0,T]
as
\delta\to\infty
.

By making use of
a bi-parameter Kolmogorov continuity criterion

[
2
, Lemma 2.1]
,
it may be possible to prove full deep-water convergence
along a continuous parameter
\delta\in(0,\infty)
.
We, however, do not pursue this issue here.
See
[
133
]
for such an argument
in the context of
the Smoluchowski-Kramers approximation for singular stochastic
wave equations.

Similar comments apply to the shallow-water convergence
considered in
Theorem
1.19
(iii).

1.6.
Stochastic modulated KdV

As the last example,
we consider
the following stochastic modulated KdV equation on the circle
with an additive white-in-time noise:

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{2}+\phi\xi,

(1.30)

where
\xi
denotes a (Gaussian) space-time white noise
whose space-time covariance is (formally) given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\xi(x_{1},t_{1})\xi(x_{2},t_{2})]=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})\delta(t_{1}-t_{2})

for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{T}
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

with
\delta
denoting the Dirac delta function.
Here,

\phi
is a Hilbert-Schimidt operator from
L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
to

H_{0}^{s}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

for some
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
where
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
denotes the projection onto non-zero frequencies.
Namely,

L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})

and
H_{0}^{s}(\mathbb{T})

are subspaces of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
consisting of mean-zero functions.

Given
\sigma\geq 0
,
11
11

11

By convention, we have
X\equiv 0
when
\sigma=0
.
Namely,
\mu_{0}=\delta_{0}
, where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac delta distribution at the trivial function.
we say that a distribution-valued random variable
X_{\sigma}
on
\mathbb{T}
(and its law, denoted by
\mu_{\sigma}
)
is a (spatial) white noise on
\mathbb{T}
with variance
\sigma
if

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma}=\Law(X_{\sigma})=\Law(\sqrt{\sigma}\,u_{0}^{\omega}),

(1.31)

where
u_{0}^{\omega}
is the white noise (with variance 1) in (
1.29
).

Let us recall the notion of
an
evolution system of measures

[
37
,
41
]

in a somewhat formal manner.
Let
\Phi_{t_{1},t_{2}}=\Phi_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{\omega}
,
t_{1}\geq t_{2}\geq 0
,
be a solution map for a given (random) dynamical system,
sending the data
\varphi
at time
t_{2}
to the solution
\Phi_{t_{1},t_{2}}\varphi
at time
t_{1}
.
Then, we define the transition semigroup
P_{t_{1},t_{2}}

by

\displaystyle P_{t_{1},t_{2}}F(\varphi)=\mathbb{E}[F(\Phi_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{\omega}\varphi)]

(1.32)

for a bounded measurable function
F
on the phase space
\mathcal{X}
.
Then, we say that
12
12

12

Strictly speaking, an evolution system of measures
is the mapping
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})
,
where
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})
denotes the family of probability measures on
\mathcal{M}
.
However, we simply refer to the family
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
of measures
as an evolution system of measures.

a family
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
of probability measures on
\mathcal{X}

is an evolution system of measures indexed by
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}F(\varphi)\rho_{t_{1}}(d\varphi)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}P_{t_{1},t_{2}}F(\varphi)\rho_{t_{2}}(d\varphi)

(1.33)

for any bounded continuous function
F
on
\mathcal{X}
and
t_{1}\geq t_{2}\geq 0
.
Note that (
1.33
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\rho_{t_{1}}=P^{*}_{t_{1},t_{2}}\rho_{t_{2}}

(1.34)

for any
t_{1}\geq t_{2}\geq 0
.
If there exists an invariant measure
\rho
,
then by setting
\rho_{t}=\rho
,
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
the family
\{\rho_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is obviously
an evolution system of measures.
It is in this sense that the notion of an evolution system
of measures is a generalization of the notion of an invariant measure.

We now state our main result on the stochastic modulated KdV (
1.30
).

Theorem 1.21
.

(i)

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy (
1.9
)
and that
\phi\in\HS(L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T});H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T}))
.
Then,
the stochastic modulated KdV equation (
1.30
)
is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

and the modulated interaction representation
\mathbf{u}(t)=U^{w}(t)^{-1}u(t)

of the solution
u

belongs to

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

for any
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
,
where
\tau>0
denotes the almost surely positive local existence time.

In particular, when
\rho>\frac{2}{3}
,
the stochastic modulated KdV equation (
1.30
)
with an additive space-time white noise
(
namely,
\phi=\Id
)

is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

(ii)

Given
\rho>\frac{2}{3}
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then,
the stochastic modulated KdV equation (
1.30
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with an additive space-time white noise

(
namely,
\phi=\Id
)

is globally well-posed with
the
(
spatial
)

white noise initial data
u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
),
independent of the space-time white noise forcing
\xi
.
Moreover, for any
t\geq 0
,
we have

\displaystyle\Law(u(t))=\mu_{1+t}.

(1.35)

Namely, the family
\{\mu_{1+t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}

is an evolution system of measures
for the stochastic modulated KdV equation (
1.30
) with the white noise initial data
u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
).

When
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
,
convergence of the Galerkin approximation (see (
9.15
)) holds
in the current stochastic setting;
see Lemma
9.4
.

The well-posedness issue of the stochastic KdV with an additive forcing:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u+u\partial_{x}u=\phi\xi

(1.36)

has
been studied on both the real line and on the torus;
see
[
42
,
43
,
108
,
114
]
.
In particular, in
[
114
]
,
Quastel, Sosoe, and the fifth author
proved an analogue of Theorem
1.21
(ii)
for (
1.36
)
with
\phi=\Id

by implementing
a variant of Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
7
]

in the context of an evolution system of measures.
The local well-posedness claim
in Theorem
1.21
(i)
follows from a slight modification
of the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i).
On the other hand,
Theorem
1.21
(ii)
follows from
a straightforward adaptation of the argument developed in
[
114
]
to the current problem.
See also
[
114
, Corollary 1.3 (ii)]

for a global existence result of (
1.36
)
with initial data of the form

w_{0}+u_{0}^{\omega}
for
w_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
based on the ideas in
[
113
]
,
which also applies to the stochastic modulated KdV (
1.30
)
with
\phi=\Id
.

The Duhamel formulation of (
1.30
) is given by

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=U^{w}(t)u_{0}+U^{w}({t})\int_{0}^{t}(U^{w}({t^{\prime}}))^{-1}\mathcal{N}(u(t^{\prime}))dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+U^{w}({t})\int_{0}^{t}(U^{w}({t^{\prime}}))^{-1}\phi\xi(dt^{\prime}),

where

U^{w}(t)=e^{-w(t)\partial_{x}^{3}}
,

\mathcal{N}(u)=\partial_{x}u^{2}
,
and
the last term on the right-hand side
represents
the effect of the stochastic forcing
\phi\xi
in (
1.30
).
As in the deterministic case,
we study the following Duhamel formulation satisfied by
the modulated interaction representation
\mathbf{u}(t)=U^{w}(t)^{-1}u(t)
:

\mathbf{u}(t)=u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{N}(U^{w}(t^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(t^{\prime}))dt^{\prime}+\Psi(t),

(1.37)

where
\Psi
is
a solution to the following stochastic equation:

\partial_{t}\Psi=U^{w}(t)^{-1}\phi\xi,\qquad\Psi(0)=0,

formally given by the following stochastic integral:

\displaystyle\Psi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}(U^{w}({t^{\prime}}))^{-1}\phi\xi(dt^{\prime}).

(1.38)

See
(
9.1
) for a precise definition of
\Psi
.
Note that, due to the roughness-in-time of the noise,
the stochastic term
\Psi

has temporal regularity
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}

(see
Lemma
9.1
)
and thus we expect that a solution
\mathbf{u}

to (
1.37
)
has temporal regularity
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
.
We point out that
our nonlinear Young integral theory
(Proposition
3.7
)
and a general local well-posedness
result
for the YDE (
1.12
)
are adapted to the case
with temporal regularity
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}

(as long as
\alpha+\gamma>1
; see the proof of Proposition
3.8
).
Hence, once we establish regularity properties
of the stochastic term
\Psi
(Lemma
9.1
),
Theorem
1.21
(i) follows
from a minor modification of the proof of
Proposition
3.8
.
See Section
9

for details.

Next, we briefly discuss a proof of
the almost sure global well-posedness result in Theorem
1.21
(ii),
based on the argument in
[
114
]
.
We first
provide a heuristic argument for the claim (
1.35
)
by viewing the stochastic modulated KdV (
1.30
)
as a
superposition of the deterministic modulated KdV (
1.2
)
and the stochastic flow:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\xi

(1.39)

(at the level of infinitesimal generators).
While
Theorem
1.18

states that
the white noise (with any variance) is invariant under (
1.2
),
it is easy to see that
the stochastic flow (
1.39
) with a white noise initial data (with any variance)
increases the variance by the length of the time interval under consideration.
Then, at a formal level,
the claim (
1.35
) follows
from these observations
together with
the
Lie-Trotter product formula
[
122
, Section VIII.8]
:

\displaystyle e^{t(A+B)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big[e^{\frac{t}{n}A}e^{\frac{t}{n}B}\big]^{n}

(1.40)

(which holds, for example,
for finite-dimensional matrices
A,B
).
Obviously,
the Lie-Trotter product formula (
1.40
)
is not directly applicable to our problem,
and we justify the heuristic argument above
by the Galerkin approximation (Lemma
9.4
).
See Section
9

for further details.

As in Subsection
1.5
,
by a straightforward modification
of the proof of Theorem
1.21
,
we obtain
the corresponding results
for the following stochastic modulated BO equation on the circle:

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{2}+\phi\xi.

(1.41)

Theorem 1.22
.

(i)

Given
\rho\geq 1
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy (
1.22
)
and that
\phi\in\HS(L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T});H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T}))
.
Then,
the stochastic modulated BO equation (
1.41
)
is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.
In particular, when
\rho>1
,
the stochastic modulated BO equation (
1.41
)
with an additive space-time white noise
(
namely,
\phi=\Id
)

is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

(ii)

Given
\rho>1
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then,
the stochastic modulated BO equation (
1.41
)
on
\mathbb{T}

with an additive space-time white noise

(
namely,
\phi=\Id
)

is globally well-posed with
the
(
spatial
)

white noise initial data
u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
),
independent of the space-time white noise forcing
\xi
.
Moreover, the family
\{\mu_{1+t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}

is an evolution system of measures
for the stochastic modulated BO equation (
1.41
) with the white noise initial data
u_{0}^{\omega}
in (
1.29
).

The same results
also hold for the
stochastic modulated ILW equation on the circle:

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}u^{2}+\phi\xi

(1.42)

and
the
stochastic modulated scaled ILW equation on the circle:

\partial_{t}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}v\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}v^{2}+\phi\xi.

(1.43)

In particular,
deep-water and shallow-water convergence
analogous to those in
Theorem
1.13

and
1.19

also holds for (
1.42
) and (
1.43
).
We omit details.

1.7.
Organization

In Section

2
,
we introduce basic notations and function spaces.
We also introduces various classes of drivers
such as
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
in (
2.9
)
which play a crucial role in the remaining part of this paper.
In Section
3
,
after providing simple criteria
for checking regularity of drivers (Lemma
3.1
),
we go over the basic theory
for nonlinear Young integrals
(Lemma
3.4
and Proposition
3.7
).
We then present a general local well-posedness
result for the YDE (
1.12
)
(Proposition
3.8
).
In Section
4
,
by establishing regularity properties
of the associated drivers,
we prove local well-posedness and related properties
for the modulated KdV-type equations.
In Section
5
,
we establish local well-posedness and related properties
for the modulated BO and ILW equations.
In Section
6
,
we prove the conservation of the
L^{2}
-norm
for the modulated dispersive equations considered in this paper
along with their
L^{2}
-global well-posedness
in the regime where they are locally well-posed in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
.
In Section
7
, we adapt the
I
-method
to the current modulated setting.
By combining a crucial commutator estimate
(Proposition
7.6
)
with the sewing lemma (Lemma
3.3
),
we prove global well-posedness of the modulated
KdV (
1.2
) in negative Sobolev spaces
(Theorems
1.3
(ii) and
1.7
(ii)).
In Section
8
, we provide a brief discussion
for
the proof of Theorem
1.18

on invariance of the white noise under the modulated KdV on the circle.
Finally,
in Section
9
,
we study the stochastic modulated KdV
(
1.30
) on the circle and prove Theorem
1.21
.

2.
Notations and preliminary tools

2.1.
Basic notations

Let
A\lesssim B
denote an estimate of the form
A\leq CB
for some constant
C>0
. We write
A\sim B
if
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
, while
A\ll B
denotes
A\leq cB
for some small constant
c>0
.
We may write
\lesssim_{\alpha}
and
\sim_{\alpha}
to
emphasize the dependence on an external parameter
\alpha
.
We use
C>0
to denote various constants, which may vary line by line.

In expressing the dependence of a function
u

on the time variable, we often use the short-hand notation

u_{t}=u(t)
, which is standard in probability theory and stochastic analysis.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{T}
.
We use
\widehat{f}
and
\mathcal{F}(f)=\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{M}}(f)

to denote
the Fourier transform
of a function
f
on
\mathcal{M}
,
defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(n)=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)e^{-inx}dx_{\mathcal{M}}.

Here, when
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
,
dx_{\mathbb{R}}=dx
denotes
the standard Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
,
while, when
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
,

dx_{\mathbb{T}}
denotes
the normalized Lebesgue measure
dx_{\mathbb{T}}=(2\pi)^{-1}dx
.
With this convention, we have

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\widehat{f}(\xi)d\xi_{\mathcal{M}^{*}},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}|f(x)|^{2}dx_{\mathcal{M}}=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}|\widehat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{M}}(fg)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\intt_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\widehat{f}(\xi_{1})\widehat{g}(\xi_{2})(d\xi_{1})_{\mathcal{M}^{*}},

where
\mathcal{M}^{*}

is the Pontryagin dual of
\mathcal{M}

(namely,
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathbb{R}
when
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
, while
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}
when
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
)
and
d\xi_{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the standard Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
,
while
d\xi_{\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}
.
On the circle, we impose the mean-zero assumption
for the equations we consider in this paper
and thus we set
\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we denote by
\mathbf{P}_{N}

the Dirichlet projector onto the frequencies

\{|\xi|\leq N\}

defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{P}_{N}f}(\xi)=\mathbf{1}_{\{|\xi|\leq N\}}\widehat{f}(\xi).

(2.1)

We set
\mathbf{P}_{N}^{\perp}=\Id-\mathbf{P}_{N}
.

2.2.
Function spaces

We use

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
to denote the space of Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}

and set

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{T})=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{T}
.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
we define the non-homogeneous and homogeneous Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})

and
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M})

via the norms:

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}|\widehat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi,

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{*}}|\xi|^{2s}|\widehat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
V,V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
be separable Hilbert spaces.
We use

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{k}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}V_{j};V\Big)

to denote
the Banach space of bounded
k
-linear operators
on
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}V_{j}

(equipped with the Hilbert tensor norm) with values in
V
.
When
V_{j}=V
for
j=1,\dots,k
,
we simply set

\mathcal{L}_{k}(V)=\mathcal{L}_{k}(V^{\otimes k};V)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space and
T>0
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote

\displaystyle\Delta_{n,T}=\big\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in[0,T]^{n}:\ t_{i}>t_{j}\text{ for }i<j\big\}.

We denote by
C_{n,T}V

the space of continuous functions
from
\Delta_{n,T}
to
V
. When
n=1
,
we may write
C_{T}V
for simplicity,
and equip this space with the supremum norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C_{T}V}=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|f(t)\|_{V}.

We define the coboundary operator

\updelta:C_{n,T}V\to C_{n+1,T}V

as follows;
given
f\in C_{n,T}V

and
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n+1})\in\Delta_{{n+1},T}
,
we set

\displaystyle(\updelta f)_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n+1}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(-1)^{n-k}f_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k-1},t_{k+1},\ldots,t_{n+1}}.

For example, for
f\in C_{T}V

and

g\in C_{2,T}V
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\updelta f)_{t,r}&=f_{t}-f_{r},\\
(\updelta g)_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}&=g_{t_{1},t_{3}}-g_{t_{1},t_{2}}-g_{t_{2},t_{3}}\end{split}

(2.2)

for
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}

and
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\Delta_{3,T}
.
As noted in
[
70
]
,
the sequence

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow C_{1,T}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\updelta}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{2,T}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\updelta}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{3,T}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\updelta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

is exact.
In particular, we have

\updelta\circ\updelta=0
and
if
f\in C_{n,T}V
with
\updelta f=0
,
then there exists a
g\in C_{n-1,T}V

such that
f=\updelta g
;
see, for example,
[
70
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Given
0<\gamma<1
, we denote by
C^{\gamma}_{T}V=C^{\gamma}([0,T];V)
the space of
\gamma
-Hölder continuous functions taking values in
V
, endowed with the seminorm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{\gamma}_{T}V}=\sup_{(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}}\frac{\|(\updelta f)_{t,r}\|_{V}}{|t-r|^{\gamma}}.

We also define

\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}_{T}V=\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,T];V)
via the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}_{T}V}=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}+\|u\|_{C^{\gamma}_{T}V}.

(2.3)

We also introduce the spaces

C^{\gamma}_{n,T}V
,
n=2,3
,
equipped with the following Hölder-type norms;
for
g\in C_{2,T}V
and
f\in C_{3,T}V
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|g\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}V}&=\sup_{(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}}\frac{\|g_{t,r}\|_{V}}{|t-r|^{\gamma}},\\
\|h\|_{C^{\gamma}_{3,T}V}&=\inf_{0<\alpha<\gamma}\sup_{(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\Delta_{3,T}}\frac{\|h_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{V}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\alpha}|t_{2}-t_{3}|^{\gamma-\alpha}}.\end{split}

(2.4)

Let
V
and
W
be Banach spaces.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
we use
\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;W)

to denote the Banach space of
locally Lipschitz maps
f:V\to W

with polynomial growth of order
k

such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;W)}=\sup_{x,y\in V}\frac{\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{W}}{\|x-y\|_{V}\big(1+\|x\|_{V}+\|y\|_{V}\big)^{k-1}}<\infty.

(2.5)

When
V=W
,
we simply set
\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)=\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;V)
.
We say that
f\in\mathrm{Lip}^{2}_{k}(V;W)

if

(i)

f\in\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;W)
,

(ii)

f
is Fréchet differentiable
with

Df\in\mathrm{Lip}_{k-1}(V;\mathcal{L}_{1}(V;W))
.

From (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;W)}=\sup_{(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}}\frac{1}{|t-r|^{\gamma}}\sup_{x,y\in V}\frac{\|f_{t,r}(x)-f_{t,r}(y)\|_{W}}{\|x-y\|_{V}\big(1+\|x\|_{V}+\|y\|_{V}\big)^{k-1}}.

(2.6)

In addition, suppose that
V_{0}\hookrightarrow V

is a Banach subspace of
V
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
we use
\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})\subset\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)

to denote the Banach space of
locally Lipschitz maps
f:V_{0}\to V_{0}

such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}=\sup_{x,y\in V}\frac{\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{V_{0}}}{G_{V,V_{0}}(x,y)}<\infty,

(2.7)

where
G_{V,V_{0}}(x,y)
is given by

\displaystyle G_{V,V_{0}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\|x-y\|_{V_{0}}\big(1+\|x\|_{V}+\|y\|_{V}\big)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\quad+\|x-y\|_{V}\big(1+\|x\|_{V}+\|y\|_{V}\big)^{k-2}\big(1+\|x\|_{V_{0}}+\|y\|_{V_{0}}\big).

Then,
from (
2.4
) and (
2.7
), we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}=\sup_{(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}}\frac{1}{|t-r|^{\gamma}}\sup_{x,y\in V}\frac{\|f_{t,r}(x)-f_{t,r}(y)\|_{V_{0}}}{G_{V,V_{0}}(x,y)}.

(2.8)

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<\gamma<1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T>0
,
we define the space

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
of drivers by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})\\
&\quad=\big\{X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}^{2}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M})):X(0)=DX[0]=0\text{ and }\updelta X=0\big\},\end{split}

(2.9)

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})}=\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))}+\|DX\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k-1}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M});\mathcal{L}_{1}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M})))}.

(2.10)

Here,
X(0)=0
(and
DX[0]=0
) means
X_{t,r}(0)=0

(and
DX_{t,r}[0]=0
, respectively)
for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
,
where
DX_{t,r}[0]

denotes the Fréchet derivative of
X_{t,r}
at
u=0\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.
Similarly, we define

\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})\\
&\quad=\big\{X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}^{2}(\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M})):X(0)=DX[0]=0\text{ and }\updelta X=0\big\},\end{split}

(2.11)

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})}=\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M}))}+\|DX\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k-1}(\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M});\mathcal{L}_{1}(\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M})))}.

(2.12)

We may simply write
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T])
or
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)
,
where there is no confusion about
an underlying spatial domain.
We write

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{+})=\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M})

if
X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)
for any
T>0

(but
\sup_{T>0}\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}
may be infinite).
A similar comment applies to the homogenous space

\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
.

More generally,
given
s,s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<\gamma<1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T>0

with
s_{0}>s
, we define the space

\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

of drivers (for establishing nonlinear smoothing) by
setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})=C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M});H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})).

(2.13)

Similarly,
we set

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})=C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M});\dot{H}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})).

In proving persistence of regularity, we need the following
class of drivers.
Given
s,s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<\gamma<1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T>0

with
s_{0}>s
, we define

\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})=C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M}),H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M});H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})).\end{split}

(2.14)

Similarly, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{\mathcal{Y}}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})=C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M}),\dot{H}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M});\dot{H}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})).\end{split}

(2.15)

We often use short-hand notations such as

C^{\gamma}_{T}H^{s}_{x}=C^{\gamma}\big([0,T],H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
,
when there is no ambiguity.

2.3.
On continuous and discrete convolutions

We recall the following basic lemma on continuous and discrete convolutions.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy

\displaystyle\alpha\geq\beta\geq 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\quad\alpha+\beta>1.

(2.16)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\frac{d\xi_{1}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle^{\beta-\lambda}},

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{1}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{\beta-\lambda}}

for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
where
\lambda=\max(1-\alpha,0)
when
\alpha\neq 1
and
\lambda=\varepsilon
when
\alpha=1
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
In particular, if (
2.16
) holds, then we have

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\frac{d\xi_{1}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{\beta}}+\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n=n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{1}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{\beta}}<\infty.

Lemma
2.1
follows
from elementary computations.
See, for example,

[
63
, Lemma 4.2]
and
[
104
, Lemma 4.1]
.

3.
Young solution theory

Our main goal in this paper is to study well-posedness of
modulated dispersive equations
in terms of the Duhamel formulation (
1.8
) satisfied
by the modulated interaction representation

\mathbf{u}(t)=U^{w}(t)^{-1}u(t)
defined in (
1.7
).
Here, the main task is to make sense of
the integral
on the right-hand side of (
1.8
).
In this section, we go over the construction of
such an integral as a nonlinear Young integral
under appropriate assumptions.

Let
p
be a function on
\mathbb{R}
, smooth on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
such that

\displaystyle p(0)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{p(i\xi)}=-p(i\xi),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

(3.1)

Namely,
p(\partial_{x})
is formally anti self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})

(which may be unbounded).
Then, our goal is to give a proper meaning to
the following integral:
