\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{N}(U^{w}(t^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(t^{\prime}))dt^{\prime},

(3.2)

where
U^{w}(t)=e^{w(t)p(\partial_{x})}
.
For this purpose, we
introduce a driver
X
, a priori defined on
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{M})
, associated with the integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})

by setting

X_{t,r}(f)=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{N}(U^{w}(t^{\prime})f)dt^{\prime},\quad t>r\geq 0.

(3.3)

In the previous work

[
28
]
,
the first and second authors introduced
an approach to study modulated dispersive equations,
where they made sense of the integral in (
3.2
)
as a nonlinear Young integral.
While our approach is equivalent
to that presented in
[
28
]
,
we construct the integral in (
3.2
)
as a nonlinear Young integral, using the sewing lemma
(Lemma
3.3
).
Moreover, we have made our presentation
in a pedestrian manner, accessible
to non-experts.

In Subsection
3.1
,
we provide simple criteria
to verify regularity of a given driver.
In
Subsection
3.2
,
we go over the construction of
a nonlinear Young integral
via the sewing lemma in an abstract setting.
In
Subsection
3.3
,
we present a basic local well-posedness
result
along with related properties
for the YDE (
1.12
)
in our functional framework.

3.1.
Regularity of drivers

As we see in the next subsection (see Proposition
3.7
),
in our functional framework,
we can construct the integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})
in (
3.2
)
with the driver
X
in (
3.3
)
as a nonlinear Young integral,
provided that
the driver
X
belongs to the class

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

defined in (
2.9
).
In this subsection, by restricting our attention to the
k
-linear setting,
we provide
simple criteria
for checking regularity of drivers.

In this paper, we consider modulated dispersive equation
with a nonlinearity
of the form:
13
13

13

For the renormalized modulated mKdV (
1.14
), the nonlinearity is not
\partial_{x}u^{3}
but can still be viewed as a trilinear operator.
See (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) below.

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(u)=\partial_{x}u^{k}

(3.4)

for an integer
k\geq 2
.
With a slight abuse of notation, by setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=\partial_{x}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k}u_{j}\bigg),

(3.5)

we can view
\mathcal{N}
as a
k
-linear operator,
a priori bounded on
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{M})
.
With this notation, we can view the driver
X
defined in (
3.3
)
as a
k
-linear operator given by

X_{t,r}(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{N}(U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{1},\dots,U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{k})dt^{\prime},\quad t>r\geq 0,

(3.6)

for functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
on
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that we have

\displaystyle X_{t,r}(f)=X_{t,r}(f,\dots,f)\quad\text{and}\quad DX_{t,r}[f](g)=kX_{t,r}(f,\dots,f,g)

(3.7)

for functions
f
and
g
on
\mathcal{M}
,
where
DX_{t,r}
denotes the Fréchet derivative of
X_{t,r}
.

In the current
k
-linear setting,
the following lemma
provides simple criteria,
allowing us to verify that
a driver
X
in (
3.3
) belongs to
the class

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

and other related classes of drivers.
Since its proof easily follows
from
(
3.7
),
the
k
-linearity of
X
in (
3.6
)
(see also (
3.5
)),
(
2.9
), (
2.10
), (
2.13
), and (
2.14
)
with
(
2.6
) and (
2.8
),
we omit details.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
X
be a driver in (
3.3
),
where
U^{w}(t)=e^{p(\partial_{x})w(t)}
with
p
as in (
3.1
)
and
\mathcal{N}
is as in (
3.4
) and (
3.5
).
Then, given
s,s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, an integer
k\geq 2
, and
T\geq 1

such that
s_{0}>s
,
the following statements hold
:

(i)

If we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
then

X
belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

defined in
(
2.9
)
with the bound

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}.

(ii)

(persistence of regularity).

Suppose that
X
can be written as
X=\sum_{j=1}^{k}X^{j}

such that for each
j=1,\dots,k
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}X_{j}&\in\mathcal{L}_{k,j}^{s,s_{0}}\\
:\!&=\mathcal{L}_{k}\Big(\big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{j-1}H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\big)\otimes H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\big(\bigotimes_{i=j+1}^{k}H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\big);H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})\Big)\end{split}

(3.8)

with the bound

\displaystyle\|X^{j}_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{k,j}^{s,s_{0}}}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}|t-r|^{\gamma}.

Then,

X
belongs to
\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

defined in (
2.14
)
with the bound

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}(T)}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}.

(iii)

(nonlinear smoothing).

If we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M});H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
then

X
belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

defined in
(
2.13
)
with the bound

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}(T)}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}.

(iv)

(convergence).

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
the truncated driver
X^{N}
by

X^{N}_{t,r}(f)=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}U^{w}(t^{\prime})f)dt^{\prime}.

If we have

\displaystyle\|X^{N}_{t,r}-X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))}\lesssim o(1)|t-r|^{\gamma},

as
N\to\infty
,
uniformly in
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
then,

X^{N}

converges to
X
in
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
.

Remark 3.2
.

(i)
While we stated Part (iv) of Lemma
3.1

for a discrete parameter
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
it also holds
for a continuous parameter.
In particular, we will use Lemma
3.1
(iv)
not only for establishing convergence of the Galerkin approximations
but also for establishing deep-water and shallow-water
convergence of the (scaled) modulated ILW (
1.17
) (and (
1.20
))
claimed in Theorem
1.13
.
See Subsections
5.3

and
5.4
.

(ii)
By replacing the non-homogeneous Sobolev spaces
with the corresponding homogeneous ones,
Lemma
3.1

yields criteria
to check if a driver
belongs to the homogeneous
version of the classes of drivers
such as

X\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

and

\dot{\mathcal{Y}}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

defined in (
2.11
)
and
in (
2.15
), respectively.

3.2.
Sewing lemma and Young integrals

In this subsection,
we go over the construction
of
the integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})
in (
3.2
)
as a nonlinear Young integral,
using the sewing lemma.
The following version of the sewing lemma
is taken from

[
70
]

with slight modifications;
see

[
70
, Proposition 2.3, Corollary 2.4, Corollary 2.5]
.
See also
[
56
, Lemma 4.2]
.

We set

C^{1+}_{n,T}V=\bigcup_{\gamma>1}C^{\gamma}_{n,T}V
.

Lemma 3.3

(sewing lemma)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space and
fix
T>0
.
Then, there exists a unique linear map
(
called the sewing map
)

\Lambda:C^{1+}_{3,T}V\cap\Ker\updelta|_{C_{3,T}V}\to C^{1+}_{2,T}V
such that

(i)

We have

\updelta\Lambda h=h

for each
h\in C_{3,T}V\cap\Ker\updelta|_{C_{3,T}V}
.

(ii)

For each
\zeta>1
,
the sewing map
\Lambda
is continuous
from
C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V\cap\Ker\updelta|_{C_{3,T}V}
to

C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V
such that

\displaystyle\|\Lambda h\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\zeta}-2}\|h\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V}

(3.9)

for any
h\in C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V
.

(iii)

Given any
g\in C_{2,T}V

with

\updelta g\in C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V
,
there exists unique

f\in C([0,T];V)

(
modulo an additive constant
)

such that

\updelta f=(\Id-\Lambda\updelta)g
.
In addition,
we have

\displaystyle(\updelta f)_{t,r}=\lim_{|\Pi([r,t])|\to 0}\sum_{j=0}^{n}g_{t_{j},t_{j+1}}

(3.10)

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
,
where
the limit is over any partition

\Pi([r,t])

of
[r,t]
:

\Pi([r,t])=\{r=t_{n}<\dots<t_{1}<t_{0}=t\}

whose mesh size

|\Pi([r,t])|=\sup_{j}|t_{j}-t_{j+1}|

tends to
0
.

As a consequence of the sewing lemma,
we have the following lemma
on the construction of nonlinear Young integrals.
See also

[
77
,
57
]
(which appeared after the first version of the current paper)
for the construction of nonlinear Young integrals
in a more general setting.

Lemma 3.4

(nonlinear Young integral)
.

Let
V
be a Banach space.
Given
0<\gamma<1
,

T>0
, and an integer
k\geq 2
,
let
X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)
, where
\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)
is as in (
2.5
), such that

\displaystyle X_{t,r}(0)=0

(3.11)

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
,
and

\displaystyle(\updelta X)_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}=0

(3.12)

for any
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\Delta_{3,T}
.
Given

f\in C^{\alpha}([0,T];V)
for some
0<\alpha<1

such that
f(0)\in V
,
define
\Theta
on
\Delta_{2,T}

by setting

\displaystyle\Theta_{t,r}=X_{t,r}(f(r)),\quad(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}.

(3.13)

Suppose that

\zeta:=\alpha+\gamma>1
,
Then, the following statements hold
:

(i)

We have
\Theta\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}V

and

\updelta\Theta\in C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V
.

(ii)

There exists
a unique function

\mathcal{I}\in C^{\gamma}([0,T];V)

such that
\mathcal{I}(0)=0
and

\displaystyle\updelta\mathcal{I}=(\Id-\Lambda\updelta)\Theta,

(3.14)

where
\Lambda
is the sewing map in Lemma
3.3
.
Moreover,
we have

\displaystyle\|\updelta\mathcal{I}-\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)},

(3.15)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}\|_{C^{\gamma}_{T}V}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)},

(3.16)

where
C_{1}(f)
and
C_{2}(f)
are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{1}(f)&=\frac{1}{2^{\zeta}-2}\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}V},\\
C_{2}(f)&=(1\vee T)^{\alpha}C_{1}(f)+\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}.\end{split}

(3.17)

Here,
a\vee b:=\max(a,b)
.
Finally, for
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
,
we have

\displaystyle(\updelta\mathcal{I})_{t,r}=\lim_{|\Pi([r,t])|\to 0}\sum^{n}_{j=0}\Theta_{t_{j},t_{j+1}},

(3.18)

where
the limit is in the sense of Lemma
3.3
(iii).
In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(t)=\lim_{|\Pi([0,t])|\to 0}\sum^{n}_{j=0}\Theta_{t_{j},t_{j+1}}.

In the following, we will refer to
\mathcal{I}

as the
nonlinear Young integral

of
f
with the driver
X

and
denote it
by
\mathcal{I}^{X}(f)
.

(iii)

(persistence of regularity).

Let
V_{0}\hookrightarrow V

be a Banach subspace of
V
.
In addition, suppose that

X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})
,
where
\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})
is as in (
2.7
)
and that

f\in C^{\alpha}([0,T];V_{0})

such that
f(0)\in V_{0}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\Theta\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}V_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\updelta\Theta\in C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V_{0}.

(3.19)

Furthermore,
we have

\mathcal{I}\in C^{\gamma}([0,T];V_{0})
,
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\updelta\mathcal{I}-\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}\|_{C^{\gamma}_{T}V_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})},

(3.21)

where
C_{3}(f)
and
C_{4}(f)
are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{3}(f)&=\frac{1}{2^{\zeta}-2}\Big(\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}V_{0}}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXX}+\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}V}\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V_{0}}\big)\Big),\\
C_{4}(f)&=(1\vee T)^{\alpha}C_{3}+2\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V_{0}}\big).\end{split}

(3.22)

(iv)

(convergence).

Suppose in addition that a sequence
\{X^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)

with
X^{N}(0)=0
and
\updelta X^{N}=0

converges to
X
in

C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)

as
N\to\infty
.
Then, given
any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];V)
,
the nonlinear Young integral
\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(f)

of
f

with the driver
X^{N}

converges to
the nonlinear Young integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(f)

of
f

with the driver
X

in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,T];V)

as
N\to\infty
.
Moreover,
given any
r>0
,
the rate of convergence is
uniform in
f\in B_{r}
,
where

B_{r}
denotes the ball
in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];V)

of radius
r>0
centered at the origin.

Remark 3.5
.

The nonlinear Young integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(f)

of
f
with a driver
X

is sometimes
denoted by

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}X_{dt^{\prime}}(f(t^{\prime})).

See, for example,

[
28
]
.

Remark 3.6
.

Let
X
be as in Lemma
3.4
.
Suppose in addition that
X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;V_{0})
,
where
V_{0}\hookrightarrow V

is a Banach subspace of
V
.
Then, given

f\in C^{\alpha}([0,T];V)
for some
0<\alpha<1

such that
\zeta=\alpha+\gamma>1
,
it follows from a straightforward modification of the proof of Lemma
3.4

that

\mathcal{I}^{X}(f)\in C^{\gamma}([0,T];V_{0})

with a bound

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(f)\|_{C^{\gamma}_{T}V_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V;V_{0})},

(3.23)

where
C_{2}(f)
is as in (
3.17
).

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

(i)
By the definition (
3.13
) of
\Theta
,
(
3.11
),
and (
2.6
),
we have

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{t,r}\|_{V}

\displaystyle=\|X_{t,r}(f_{r})-X_{t,r}(0)\|_{V}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\leq\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t-r|^{\gamma}\big(1+\|f_{r}\|_{V}\big)^{k-1}\|f_{r}\|_{V}

\displaystyle\leq A_{1}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t-r|^{\gamma},

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
,
where
A_{1}(f)
is given by

\displaystyle A_{1}(f)=\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}<\infty.

(3.25)

This proves

\Theta\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}V
,
since
X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)
.

From (
3.13
) and (
3.12
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\updelta\Theta)_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}&=X_{t_{1},t_{3}}(f_{t_{3}})-X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(f_{t_{2}})-X_{t_{2},t_{3}}(f_{t_{3}})\\
&=X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(f_{t_{3}})-X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(f_{t_{2}}),\end{split}

(3.26)

for any
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\Delta_{3,T}
.
Then, from (
2.6
)
and the
\alpha
-Hölder continuity of
f
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\updelta\Theta)_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{V}&\leq\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}\big(1+\|f_{t_{2}}\|_{V}+\|f_{t_{3}}\|_{V}\big)^{k-1}\\
&\quad\times\|f_{t_{2}}-f_{t_{3}}\|_{V}\\
&\leq A_{2}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}|t_{2}-t_{3}|^{\alpha},\end{split}

(3.27)

where
A_{2}(f)
is given by

\displaystyle A_{2}(f)

\displaystyle=\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}V}.

(3.28)

Consequently,
from (
2.4
)
and
\zeta=\alpha+\gamma
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\updelta\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V}\leq A_{2}\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}<\infty,

(3.29)

yielding

\updelta\Theta\in C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V
.

(ii)
In view of (i) with
\zeta=\alpha+\gamma>1
,
we apply the sewing lemma (Lemma
3.3
)
and conclude that
there exists
\mathcal{I}\in C([0,T];V)

such that

\updelta\mathcal{I}=(\Id-\Lambda\updelta)\Theta
.
Moreover, from (
3.14
) and (
3.9
), we have

\displaystyle\|\updelta\mathcal{I}-\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V}=\|\Lambda(\updelta\Theta)\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\zeta}-2}\|\updelta\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V}.

(3.30)

Hence,
by setting
C_{1}(f)=\frac{1}{2^{\zeta}-2}A_{2}(f)
,
the bound (
3.15
)
follows from (
3.30
)
and
(
3.29
).

From
(
3.15
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}_{t}-\mathcal{I}_{r}-\Theta_{t,r}\|_{V}\leq C_{1}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t-r|^{\zeta},

(3.31)

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
.
Then, from (
3.31
), (
3.24
),
and the triangle inequality,
we conclude that

\mathcal{I}\in C^{\gamma}_{T}V
, satisfying the bound
(
3.16
).
Lastly, we note that
the identity (
3.18
) follows from (
3.10
).

(iii)
Proceeding as in (
3.24
)
with (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{t,r}\|_{V_{0}}

\displaystyle=\|X_{t,r}(f_{r})-X_{t,r}(0)\|_{V_{0}}

(3.32)

\displaystyle\leq A_{3}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
,
where
A_{3}=A_{3}(f)
is given by

\displaystyle A_{3}(f)=2\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\big(1+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V_{0}}\big)<\infty.

This proves

\Theta\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}V_{0}
,
since
X\in C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})
.
Proceeding as in (
3.27
) with (
3.26
) and (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|(\updelta\Theta)_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{V_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big\{\big(1+\|f_{t_{2}}\|_{V}+\|f_{t_{3}}\|_{V}\big)^{k-1}\|f_{t_{2}}-f_{t_{3}}\|_{V_{0}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXX}+\big(1+\|f_{t_{2}}\|_{V}+\|f_{t_{3}}\|_{V}\big)^{k-2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXX}\times\big(1+\|f_{t_{2}}\|_{V_{0}}+\|f_{t_{3}}\|_{V_{0}}\big)\|f_{t_{2}}-f_{t_{3}}\|_{V}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq A_{4}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}|t_{2}-t_{3}|^{\alpha},

where
A_{4}(f)
is given by

\displaystyle A_{4}(f)

\displaystyle=\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-1}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}V_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V}\big)^{k-2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{T}V}\big(1+2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}V_{0}}\big).

Consequently,
from (
2.4
)
and
\zeta=\alpha+\gamma
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\updelta\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V_{0}}\leq A_{4}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}<\infty,

(3.33)

yielding

\updelta\Theta\in C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V_{0}
.
This proves (
3.19
).
Then, from
the sewing lemma (Lemma
3.3
),
we obtain
\mathcal{I}\in C([0,T];V_{0})
.
Proceeding as in (
3.30
),
the bound (
3.20
)
with
C_{3}(f)=\frac{1}{2^{\zeta}-2}A_{4}(f)

follows from (
3.9
) and (
3.33
).
From
(
3.20
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}_{t}-\mathcal{I}_{r}-\Theta_{t,r}\|_{V_{0}}\leq C_{3}(f)\|X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V,V_{0};V_{0})}|t-r|^{\zeta},

(3.34)

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
.
Then,
the bound (
3.21
) follows from (
3.34
), (
3.32
),
and the triangle inequality.

(iv)
Let
\mathcal{I}^{N}(f)=\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(f)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathcal{I}(f)=\mathcal{I}^{X}(f)

be the nonlinear Young integrals of
f

associated with the drivers

X^{N}
and
X
, respectively,
constructed in Part (i).
Then,
from (
3.14
) and the linearity of the sewing map
\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\updelta\mathcal{I}^{N}(f)-\updelta\mathcal{I}(f)=\Theta^{N}-\Theta-\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{N}-\updelta\Theta),

(3.35)

where
\Theta
is as in (
3.13
) and
\Theta^{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta_{t,r}^{N}=X^{N}_{t,r}(f_{r}),\quad(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}.

(3.36)

Proceeding as in (
3.24
),
we have

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{t,r}^{N}-\Theta_{t,r}\|_{V}\leq A_{1}(f)\|X^{N}-X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t-\gamma|^{\gamma},

(3.37)

where
A_{1}(f)
is as in (
3.25
).
On the other hand,
from (
3.9
) in Lemma
3.3
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{N}-\updelta\Theta))_{t,r}\|_{V}&\leq|t-r|^{\zeta}\|\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{N}-\updelta\Theta)\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}V}\\
&\lesssim|t-r|^{\zeta}\|\updelta\Theta^{N}-\updelta\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,T}V}.\end{split}

(3.38)

Proceeding as in (
3.27
)
with (
3.36
)
and (
3.26
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\updelta&\Theta^{N}_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}-\updelta\Theta_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{V}\\
&=\big\|(X^{N}_{t_{1},t_{2}}-X_{t_{1},t_{2}})(f_{t_{3}})-(X^{N}_{t_{1},t_{2}}-X_{t_{1},t_{2}})(f_{t_{2}})\big\|_{V}\\
&\leq A_{2}(f)\|X^{N}-X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}|t_{2}-t_{3}|^{\alpha},\end{aligned}

(3.39)

where
A_{2}(f)
is as in
(
3.28
).
Thus, from (
3.38
) and (
3.39
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|(\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{N}-\updelta\Theta))_{t,r}\|_{V}

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}(f)\|X^{N}-X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}|t-r|^{\zeta}.

(3.40)

Hence, from (
3.35
), (
3.37
), and (
3.40
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\updelta(\mathcal{I}^{N}(f))_{t,r}-\updelta(\mathcal{I}(f))_{t,r}\|_{V}\\
&\quad\leq\Big(A_{1}(f)|t-r|^{\gamma}+A_{2}(f)|t-r|^{\zeta}\Big)\|X^{N}-X\|_{C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(V)}.\end{split}

(3.41)

from which
we conclude
that
\mathcal{I}^{N}(f)
converges
to
\mathcal{I}(f)
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,T];V)
.

Give
r>0
, it follows from (
3.25
)
and
(
3.28
)
that

A_{1}(f)
and
A_{2}(f)

are uniformly bounded
for
f\in B_{r}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];V)
.
Then, together with (
3.41
),
we conclude that
the rate of convergence is
uniform in
f\in B_{r}
.
∎

In the following proposition,
we summarize
the properties
of the nonlinear Young integral

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})

for a driver

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}

and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<\gamma<1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T>0
,
let
X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
,
where
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
is defined in (
2.9
).
Let

0<\alpha<1

such that

\zeta=\alpha+\gamma>1
.

(i)

Let

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.
Then, the nonlinear Young integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})

with the driver
X
exists
as the unique function

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})\in C^{\gamma}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

with
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(0)=0

such that

\displaystyle\updelta\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})=(\Id-\Lambda\updelta)\Theta,

(3.42)

where
\Theta
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta_{t,r}=X_{t,r}(\mathbf{u}(r)),\quad(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|\updelta\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})-\Theta\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,T}H^{s}_{x}}\leq C_{1}(\mathbf{u})\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)},

(3.43)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})\|_{C^{\gamma}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}\leq C_{2}(\mathbf{u})\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)},

(3.44)

where
C_{1}(\mathbf{u})
and
C_{2}(\mathbf{u})
are as in (
3.17
)
with
V=H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
,
and

\displaystyle(\updelta\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}))_{t,r}=\lim_{|\Pi([r,t])|\to 0}\sum^{n}_{j=0}\Theta_{t_{j},t_{j+1}}

for any
(t,r)\in\Delta_{2,T}
.

(ii)

Suppose that a sequence
\{X^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

converges to
X
in

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
as
N\to\infty
.
Then, given
any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
,
the nonlinear Young integral
\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})

of
\mathbf{u}

with the driver
X^{N}

converges to
the nonlinear Young integral
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})

of
\mathbf{u}

with the driver
X

in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

as
N\to\infty
.
Moreover,
given any
r>0
,
the rate of convergence is
uniform in
\mathbf{u}\in B_{r}

where

B_{r}
denotes the ball
in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

of radius
r>0
centered at the origin.

Proof.

By noting from (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) that

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})\subset C^{\gamma}_{2,T}\mathrm{Lip}_{k}(H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
,
the claims follow from Lemma
3.4
with
V=H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.
∎

3.3.
Young differential equation

Our goal in this paper is to
study the
following YDE:

\mathbf{u}(t)=u_{0}+\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t),

(3.45)

where
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})
is the nonlinear Young integral of

\mathbf{u}
with a driver
X
of the form (
3.3
).
In the following proposition,
we establish local well-posedness
and related properties
of
the YDE (
3.45
) in the abstract setting with a driver
X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
.
We refer interested readers
to
[
57
]
for a recent review on
nonlinear YDEs.
In Sections
4

and
5
,
by verifying the hypotheses
in Lemma
3.1
,
we apply Proposition
3.8

to prove local well-posedness
and other related properties
of modulated dispersive equations.

Proposition 3.8
.

(i) (local well-posedness).

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T\geq 1
,
let
X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
,
where
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
is defined in (
2.9
).
Then,
the Young differential equation (
3.45
) with
the driver
X

is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.
More precisely, given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
,
there exist
C_{0}>0
and
\theta>0
, independent of
u_{0}
and
X
,
and
a unique solution
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

to
(
3.45
)
with
\mathbf{u}|_{t=0}=u_{0}
, where the local existence time
\tau=\tau\big(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})},\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big)\in(0,1]

satisfies

\displaystyle\tau\geq C_{0}\Big(\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}(\mathcal{M})})^{k-1}\Big)^{-\theta}.

(3.46)

Moreover, given
0<\alpha<\gamma
,
there exists
C_{\alpha}>0

such that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\leq C_{\alpha}\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}},

(3.47)

while we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}})^{k}.

(3.48)

(ii) (persistence of regularity).

In addition, suppose that
u_{0}\in H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
for some
s_{0}>s

and that
X\in\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
,
where
\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
is as in (
2.14
).
Then,
by possibly making
\tau>0
smaller by a multiplicative constant

(
still satisfying (
3.46
)
;
in particular
\tau>0

depends on the
H^{s}
-norm of the initial data
u_{0}

but not on its
H^{s_{0}}
-norm
)
,
we have
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))
.

(iii) (nonlinear smoothing)

In addition to the hypotheses in Part (i),
suppose that
X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
,
where
\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
is as in (
2.13
).
Then, we have

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))
,
where
\mathbf{u}
is the solution to (
3.45
)
constructed in Part (i).

(iv) (convergence)

In addition to the hypotheses in Part (i),
suppose that a sequence
\{X^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

converges to
X
in

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
as
N\to\infty
.
Then,
by possibly making
\tau>0
smaller by a multiplicative constant

(
still satisfying (
3.46
)
)
, the solution

\mathbf{u}^{N}
to the following Young differential equation
:

\mathbf{u}^{N}(t)=u_{0}+\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u}^{N})(t)

(3.49)

converges to the solution
\mathbf{u}
to (
3.45
)
constructed in Part (i)
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

as
N\to\infty
.
Moreover,
given any
r>0
,
the rate of convergence of
\mathbf{u}^{N}
to
\mathbf{u}
is
uniform in
u_{0}\in B_{r}

where

B_{r}
denotes the ball
in
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})

of radius
r>0
centered at the origin.

In Proposition
3.8
,
we assumed
T\geq 1

for simplicity.
With the same proof,
we have the same results even when
0<T<1
,
in which case,
the local existence time
\tau>0

needs to satisfy an additional constraint
\tau\leq T
.

Remark 3.9
.

(i)
Suppose that
X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M})
.
Then, from (
3.46
), we obtain the following blowup alternative;
let
T_{*}\in(0,\infty]
be the maximal time of existence
of the solution
\mathbf{u}
to the YDE (
3.45
).
Then, we have either

\displaystyle T_{*}=\infty\qquad\text{or}\qquad\lim_{t\nearrow T_{*}}\|u(t)\|_{H^{s}}=\infty.

(3.50)

(ii)
In Proposition
3.8
, we presented
a local well-posedness result
for the YDE (
3.45
)
in the non-homogeneous Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.
Given

X\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})

defined in (
2.11
)
and

u_{0}\in\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
,
the same proof yields
the corresponding local well-posedness
result in the
homogeneous Sobolev spaces.

(iii)
Given
\rho>0
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
and
w_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
Let
X=X^{w}
and
X^{n}=X^{w_{n}}
be
the associated drivers defined in (
3.3
)
with the nonlinearity of the form (
3.4
).
Suppose that convergence of
w_{n}
to
w
in

\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}
implies
convergence of

X^{n}
to
X
in

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
.
Then,
from (the proof of) Proposition
3.8
,
we see that
the solution map:

(u_{0},w)\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}\longmapsto\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

is continuous.
In our application,
convergence of
w_{n}
to
w
in

\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}
indeed implies
convergence of the corresponding driver
X^{n}
to
X

in

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
.
See
Remark
4.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

(i)
Let
0<\alpha<\gamma
such that

\alpha+\gamma>1
.
Given
0<\tau\leq 1
(to be chosen later),
define
the map

\Gamma=\Gamma_{X,u_{0}}
on
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

by setting

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathbf{u})(t)=u_{0}+\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t),

(3.51)

where
\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})
is the nonlinear Young integral of

\mathbf{u}
with a driver

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
,
constructed in Proposition
3.7
.
We show that
\Gamma
is a contraction on
the ball
B_{R}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

of radius
R>0

(to be chosen later)
centered at the origin.

Recalling that
\alpha+\gamma>1
,
it follows
from (
3.44
) in
Proposition
3.7

with (
3.17
)) that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t)-\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(r)\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}|t-r|^{\gamma}\big(1+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\big)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq\tau
.
Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})\|_{C^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\tau^{\gamma-\alpha}\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big(1+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\big)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}.

(3.52)

Hence, from (
3.51
)
and (
3.52
) with
(
2.3
) and

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(0)=0
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(\mathbf{u})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+C\tau^{\gamma-\alpha}\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big(1+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\big)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}

(3.53)

for any
0<\tau\leq 1
.
Then, by setting
14
14

14

In the following, we assume that
u_{0}\neq 0
.
If
u_{0}=0
, we can simply set
R=1
and carry out the contraction argument
to show that
\mathbf{u}\equiv 0
is a unique solution to (
3.51
).

\displaystyle R=2\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}}

(3.54)

and choosing

\tau=\tau\big(R,\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big)=\tau\big(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}},\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big)>0

sufficiently small,
we see that

\Gamma
maps the ball
B_{R}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

of radius
R>0

centered at the origin into itself.

Next, we show that
\Gamma
is a contraction.
Given
\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2}\in B_{R}
, let

\displaystyle\Theta_{t,r}^{1}=X_{t,r}(\mathbf{u}^{1}_{r})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Theta_{t,r}^{2}=X_{t,r}(\mathbf{u}^{2}_{r}).

(3.55)

Then,
from (
3.51
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{1})-\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{2})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}=\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}^{1})-\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}^{2})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}

(3.56)

for any
0<\tau\leq 1
.
From (
3.42
) and the linearity of
\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\updelta(\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}^{1}))_{t,r}-\updelta(\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}^{2}))_{t,r}\|_{H^{s}}\\
&\quad\leq\|\Theta^{1}_{t,r}-\Theta^{2}_{t,r}\|_{H^{s}}+\|(\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{1}-\updelta\Theta^{2}))_{t,r}\|_{H^{s}}\end{split}

(3.57)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq\tau
.
From (
3.55
) and (
2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Theta^{1}_{t,r}-\Theta^{2}_{t,r}\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}|t-r|^{\gamma}

(3.58)

\displaystyle\times\big(1+\|\mathbf{u}^{1}_{r}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{u}^{2}_{r}\|_{H^{s}}\big)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}^{1}_{r}-\mathbf{u}^{2}_{r}\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}|t-r|^{\gamma}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}^{1}-\mathbf{u}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{a}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq\tau

and
\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2}\in B_{R}
.

Now, let us estimate the second term on the right-hand side
of (
3.57
).
Let
\zeta=\alpha+\gamma>1
.
Then, from (
3.9
) in Lemma
3.3
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{1}-\updelta\Theta^{2}))_{t,r}\|_{H^{s}}&\leq|t-r|^{\zeta}\|\Lambda(\updelta\Theta^{1}-\updelta\Theta^{2})\|_{C^{\zeta}_{2,\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim|t-r|^{\zeta}\|\updelta\Theta^{1}-\updelta\Theta^{2}\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,\tau}H^{s}_{x}}.\end{split}

(3.59)

From (
3.55
),
(
3.26
),
and the fundamental theorem of calculus,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\updelta&\Theta^{1}_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}-\updelta\Theta^{2}_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{H^{s}}\\
&=\big\|X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{3}})-X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{3}})-\big(X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2}})-X_{t_{1},t_{2}}(\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2}})\big)\big\|_{H^{s}}\\
&=\bigg\|\int^{1}_{0}DX_{t_{1},t_{2}}[\lambda\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{3}}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{3}}](\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{3}}-\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{3}})d\lambda\\
&\hphantom{XX}-\int^{1}_{0}DX_{t_{1},t_{2}}[\lambda\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2}}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2}}](\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2}})d\lambda\bigg\|_{H^{s}}\end{aligned}

(3.60)

for any
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\Delta_{3,\tau}
, where

DX_{t_{1},t_{2}}
denotes the
Fréchet derivative of
X_{t_{1},t_{2}}
.
Then, from (
2.10
) with (
2.5
),
we bound the right-hand side of (
3.60
)
by

\displaystyle\bigg\|

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{0}DX_{t_{1},t_{2}}[\lambda\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{3}}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{3}}]\big((\updelta\mathbf{u}^{1})_{t_{2},t_{3}}-(\updelta\mathbf{u}^{2})_{t_{2},t_{3}}\big)d\lambda\bigg\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg\|\int^{1}_{0}\Big(DX_{t_{1},t_{2}}[\lambda\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{3}}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{3}}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXXX}-DX_{t_{1},t_{2}}[\lambda\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2}}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2}}]\Big)(\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2}})d\lambda\bigg\|_{H^{s}}

(3.61)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}(1+R)^{k-1}\|(\updelta\mathbf{u}^{1})_{t_{2},t_{3}}-(\updelta\mathbf{u}^{2})_{t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\quad+\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}(1+R)^{k-2}\big(\|\updelta\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\updelta\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2},t_{3}}\|_{H^{s}}\big)\|\mathbf{u}^{1}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{u}^{2}_{t_{2}}\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\gamma}|t_{2}-t_{3}|^{\alpha}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}^{1}-\mathbf{u}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}

for

\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2}\in B_{R}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.
Thus, from (
3.60
) and (
3.61
) with
\zeta=\alpha+\gamma>1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\updelta(\Theta^{1}-\Theta^{2})\|_{C^{\zeta}_{3,\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}^{1}-\mathbf{u}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}.

(3.62)

Hence, putting
(
3.56
), (
3.57
),
(
3.58
), (
3.59
), and (
3.62
)
together,
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{1})-\Gamma(\mathbf{u}^{2})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}&=\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}^{1})-\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u}^{2})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\tau^{\gamma-\alpha}\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}^{1}-\mathbf{u}^{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}.\end{split}

(3.63)

Then, by
choosing

\tau=\tau\big(R,\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big)=\tau\big(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}},\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\big)>0

sufficiently small,
we conclude that
\Gamma
is a contraction on
B_{R}
.
The bound (
3.46
) follows from (
3.53
) and (
3.63
).

By the Banach fixed point theorem,
there exists a unique fixed point
\mathbf{u}\in B_{R}

such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}(t)=\Gamma(\mathbf{u})(t)=u_{0}+\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t).

(3.64)

Namely,

\mathbf{u}
is a solution to the YDE (
3.45
).
Here, the uniqueness a priori holds only in the ball
B_{R}

but, by a standard continuity argument,
we can extend uniqueness to the entire space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

(by possibly making
\tau
smaller by a multiplicative constant).
Moreover, from Proposition
3.7
,
we know that the right-hand side of (
3.64
)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
,
from which we conclude that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.

Finally the bound (
3.47
)
follows from the fact that
\mathbf{u}\in B_{R}
with (
3.54
),
while
the bound (
3.48
)
follows from
(
3.64
),
(
3.44
), and (
3.17
)
with the fact that
\mathbf{u}\in B_{R}
and (
3.54
).

(ii)
In Part (i), we constructed a solution to (
3.45
)
on the time interval
[0,\tau]
with the bound

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\leq R.

Proceeding as in (
3.52
) and (
3.53
) with
(
3.64
),
(
3.21
), (
3.22
),
and (
2.14
),
we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s_{0}}_{x}}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}+C\tau^{\gamma-\alpha}\|X\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{C^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s_{0}}_{x}}.

Then, by possibly making
\tau>0
smaller (by a multiplicative constant, and hence (
3.46
)
still holds),
we conclude that

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))
.
Then, from
Lemma
3.4
(iii),
we obtain

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))

and thus

\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))

in view of (
3.64
).

(iii)
Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
be the solution constructed in Part (i).
Then, since
2\gamma>1
,
it follows from
Remark
3.6

that

\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))
.

(iv)
For simplicity, we assume that
X\neq 0
.
We first note that the convergence of
X^{N}
to
X
in

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}([0,T]\times\mathcal{M})
implies
that there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\|X^{N}\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}\leq 2\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}

(3.65)

for any
N\geq N_{0}
.
Thus, by making
\tau
smaller by a multiplicative constant,
Part (i) guarantees existence of
a unique solution
\mathbf{u}^{N}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

to (
3.49
) for each
N\geq N_{0}
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}^{N}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}},\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\leq R

(3.66)

for any
N\geq N_{0}
, where
R
is as in Part (i).

From (
3.45
) and (
3.49
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{u}(t)-\mathbf{u}^{N}(t)&=\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t)-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u}^{N})(t)\\
&=\Big(\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})(t)-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})(t)\Big)+\Big(\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})(t)-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u}^{N})(t)\Big).\end{split}

(3.67)

From
Proposition
3.7
(ii),
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to 0}\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}=0.

(3.68)

By applying
(
3.57
),
(
3.58
), (
3.59
), and (
3.62
)
with (
3.65
) and (
3.66
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u}^{N})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\tau^{\gamma-\alpha}\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{N}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}

(3.69)

for any
N\geq N_{0}
.
Hence,
by possibly taking
\tau>0
smaller (by a multiplicative constant),
it follows
from (
3.67
),
(
3.68
),
and (
3.69
) that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{N}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathcal{I}^{X}(\mathbf{u})-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0,

(3.70)

as
N\to\infty
.
This proves convergence of
\mathbf{u}^{N}
to
\mathbf{u}
in

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.

We now upgrade this convergence to that
in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
.
By applying (
3.57
),
(
3.58
), (
3.59
), and (
3.62
)
with (
3.65
) and (
3.66
)
once again,
we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u})-\mathcal{I}^{X^{N}}(\mathbf{u}^{N})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{k}(T)}(1+R)^{k-1}\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{N}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau}H^{s}_{x}}.

(3.71)

Then, the claimed convergence of
\mathbf{u}^{N}
to
\mathbf{u}

in
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

follows from (
3.67
),
(
3.68
), and (
3.71
) with (
3.70
).
Moreover, given
r>0
,
the uniform convergence rate for
\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}}\leq r

follows
from the fact that the
rate of the convergence (
3.68
)
is uniform in

\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}}\leq r

(Proposition
3.7
(ii))
and the bound (
3.66
)
which is uniform with respect to the initial data

u_{0}

with
\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}}\leq r
.

This completes the proof of Proposition
3.8
.
∎

4.
Local well-posedness
of the modulated KdV and mKdV

In this section, we prove local well-posedness
and related properties
(persistence of regularity, nonlinear smoothing, and convergence of the Galerkin approximation)
of the modulated KdV-type equations:

•

Theorem
1.3
(i) and Theorem
1.14

for the modulated
KdV (
1.2
)
on the circle,

•

Theorems
1.9
and
1.15

for the modulated
mKdV (
1.13
)
on the circle,

•

Theorem
1.7
(i)
for the modulated KdV (
1.2
)
on the real line.

These results follow
from
Proposition
3.8
,
once we verify the conditions
in
Lemma
3.1

for each of the equations.

4.1.
Modulated KdV equation on the circle

We consider the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on
\mathbb{T}
.
More precisely, as we discussed in Section
1
,
we consider the following equation satisfied by
the modulated interaction representation
\mathbf{u}

of
u
defined in (
1.7
):

\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}=U^{w}(t)^{-1}\partial_{x}\big((U^{w}(t)\mathbf{u})^{2}\big),

(4.1)

where

U^{w}(t)=e^{-w(t)\partial_{x}^{3}}

denotes the modulated linear propagator for (
1.2
).
By writing (
4.1
) (with initial data
u_{0}
) in the integral form, we have

\mathbf{u}(t)=u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\partial_{x}\big((U^{w}(t)\mathbf{u})^{2}\big)dt^{\prime}.

(4.2)

Then,
bilinear driver
X=X^{\text{\rm KdV}}
associated with the modulated KdV is
given by

\displaystyle X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})=X^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\partial_{x}\big((U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{1})(U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{2})\big)dt^{\prime}

(4.3)

for functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
on
\mathbb{T}
.
By taking the Fourier transform, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big(X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\big)(n)=in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n}))\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2}),

(4.4)

where
\Phi^{w}_{t,r}
is as in (
1.6
)
and
\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n})
denotes the
resonance function
15
15

15

Here, we follow
the terminology in
[
131
]
.
We point out that
\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n})
is also called the modulation function (see
[
91
]
)
but we do not use this latter terminology to avoid
confusion with a modulation function
w(t)
.

for KdV given by

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n})

\displaystyle=\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(n,n_{1},n_{2})=-n^{3}+n_{1}^{3}+n_{2}^{3}.

(4.5)

Recall that, under
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n})=-3nn_{1}n_{2}.

(4.6)

The following proposition establishes
basic mapping properties
of the bilinear driver
X
defined in (
4.3
).

Proposition 4.1
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.9
).
Then,
the driver
X
defined in (
4.3
)
belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.9
).

(ii)

(persistence of regularity).

Suppose that
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.9
).
Then,
for any
s_{0}>s
,
the driver
X

belongs to

\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.14
).

(iii)

(nonlinear smoothing).

In addition, suppose that
s_{0}>s
satisfies
(
1.24
).
Then,
the driver
X

belongs to

\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.13
).

(iv)

(Galerkin approximation).

Suppose that
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.25
).
Then,
the truncated driver
X^{N}=X^{\text{\rm KdV},N}
, defined by

\displaystyle X_{t,r}^{N}(f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{N}\partial_{x}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{1})(\mathbf{P}_{N}U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{2})\big)dt^{\prime},

(4.7)

converges
to
X

in
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
as
N\to\infty
.

Once we prove Proposition
4.1
,
local well-posedness of the modulated KdV (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{T}

and other related properties
(Theorem
1.3
(i) and Theorem
1.14
) follow
from Proposition
3.8

with Proposition
4.1

applied to (
4.2
).
We omit details.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

In the following, we verify the hypotheses
in Lemma
3.1

with
k=2
.
Then, the claimed results follow from
Lemma
3.1
.

(i)
From (
4.4
)
and Hölder’s inequality,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\|_{H^{s}}^{2}&\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2s+2}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n}))\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2})\bigg|^{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2s+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n}))|^{2}}{\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}}|\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})|^{2}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\\
&\leq\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1}=n-n_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle n\rangle^{2s+2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n}))|^{2}}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}}\bigg)\|f_{1}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\end{aligned}

(4.8)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
.
Then, from
(
4.8
),
(
1.5
), and
(
4.6
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s})}&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1}=n-n_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle n\rangle^{2-4\rho}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{aligned}

(4.9)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
.
Without loss of generality,
assume that
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n_{2}|
in (
4.9
).
Then, we have
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|
under
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1}=n-n_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle n\rangle^{2-4\rho}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}\lesssim\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{4\rho-2}\langle n-n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}},

(4.10)

provided that

s+\rho\geq 0
.
In view of Lemma
2.1
,
we see that the right-hand side of (
4.10
)
is finite if

\displaystyle 4\rho-2\geq 0,\quad 2s+2\rho\geq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad 4\rho-2+2s+2\rho>1,

Namely,

\displaystyle\rho\geq\frac{1}{2},\quad s\geq-\rho,\quad\text{and}\quad s>\frac{3}{2}-3\rho,

(4.11)

which is satisfied under (
1.9
).
Hence,
from Lemma
3.1
(i),
we conclude that

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
under (
1.9
).

(ii) Write
X=X^{1}+X^{2}
,
where
X^{1}
is the contribution from
|n_{1}|\geq|n_{2}|
in (
4.4
)
and
X^{2}=X-X^{1}
.
We only consider
X^{1}
since a similar bound holds for
X^{2}
by symmetry.
Proceeding as in (
4.8
), (
4.9
), and (
4.10
)
with
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|

and (
3.8
),
we have

\displaystyle\|X^{1}_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2,1}^{s,s_{0}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{4\rho-2}\langle n-n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that (
1.9
) holds.
Hence,
from Lemma
3.1
(ii),
we conclude that, if (
1.9
) holds,
then

X\in\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

for any
s_{0}>s
.

(iii) Without loss of generality,
let us assume
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|
in (
4.4
).
Then, proceeding as in Part (i),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s};H^{s_{0}})}&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}\\
&\quad\times\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{4\rho-2s_{0}+2s-2}\langle n-n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{aligned}

(4.12)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
.
In view of Lemma
2.1
,
we see that the right-hand side of (
4.12
)
is finite if

\displaystyle s_{0}\leq s+2\rho-1,\quad s\geq-\rho,\quad\text{and}\quad s_{0}<2s+3\rho-\frac{3}{2},

which is satisfied under (
1.24
).
Hence,
from Lemma
3.1
(iii),
we conclude that

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
under (
1.24
).

(iv)
From (
4.4
) with (
4.7
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big(X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\big)(n)-\mathcal{F}\big(X^{N}_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\big)(n)

\displaystyle\quad=in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\max(|n|,|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|)>N\}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{n}))\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2}).

Note that

\mathbf{1}_{\{\max(|n|,|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|)>N\}}\lesssim N^{-\varepsilon}\max(|n|,|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|)^{\varepsilon}.

(4.13)

Assume
that
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n_{2}|
as in Part (i)
(which implies
|n_{1}|\sim\max(|n|,|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|)
),
Then, by proceeding as in (
4.9
) and (
4.10
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|X_{t,r}-X^{N}_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s})}\\
&\quad\lesssim N^{-\varepsilon}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1}=n-n_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle n\rangle^{2-4\rho+2\varepsilon}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s+2\rho-2\varepsilon}}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho-2\varepsilon}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\quad\lesssim N^{-\varepsilon}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{4\rho-2-2\varepsilon}\langle n-n_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{aligned}

(4.14)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that

s+\rho\geq\varepsilon
.
Moreover,
in view of Lemma
2.1
,
we see that the last factor on the right-hand side of (
4.14
)
is finite if

\displaystyle\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon,\quad s\geq-\rho,\quad\text{and}\quad s>\frac{3}{2}-3\rho,

which is satisfied under (
1.25
).
Hence,
from Lemma
3.1
(iii),
we conclude that

X^{N}
converges to
X
in

\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
under (
1.25
)
by choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
and
w_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
Let
X=X^{w}
and
X^{n}=X^{w_{n}}
be
the bilinear drivers
for the modulated KdV on the circle
defined in (
4.3
)
associated with the modulations
w
and
w_{n}
, respectively.
Let
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.9
)
such that Proposition
4.1
(i) guarantees

X,X^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
.
Now, suppose that
w_{n}
converges to
w
in

\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, a straightforward modification of
(
4.8
) and (
4.9
) with the finiteness of the sum
on the right-hand side of (
4.9
)
under (
1.9
) yields

\displaystyle\|X^{n}_{t,r}-X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w_{n}}-\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}=o(1)|t-r|^{\gamma}

(4.15)

as
n\to\infty
.
Hence, we conclude from Lemma
3.1
(iv)
that

X^{n}
converges to
X

in
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

with the Lipschitz bound (
4.15
).
Then, from Remark
3.9
(iii)
with (
3.41
) and(
4.15
),
we see that the solution map:

(u_{0},w)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\times\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}\longmapsto\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

is in fact locally Lipschitz continuous.
A similar comment applies
to all the modulated dispersion equations
we consider in this paper.

4.2.
Modulated mKdV equation on the circle

In this subsection,
we consider the modulated (renormalized) mKdV (
1.14
) on
\mathbb{T}
.
The renormalized nonlinearity in (
1.14
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(f)=3\big(f^{2}-\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)\partial_{x}f.

(4.16)

By taking the Fourier transform and symmetrization, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{N}(f)}(n)

\displaystyle=in\sum_{A_{n}}\widehat{f}(n_{1})\widehat{f}(n_{2})\widehat{f}(n_{3})-3in|\widehat{f}(n)|^{2}\widehat{f}(n),

(4.17)

where

\displaystyle A_{n}=\big\{(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{3}:n=n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3},\ n\neq n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\big\}.

(4.18)

See

[
6
, Lemma 8.16]

and
[
129
, (1.9)]
.
With a slight abuse of notation,
define
the trilinear version of
\mathcal{N}(f)
by
setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\big(\mathcal{N}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\big)(n)&=in\sum_{A_{n}}\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2})\widehat{f}_{3}(n_{3})\\
&\quad-3in\widehat{f}_{1}(n)\overline{\widehat{f}_{2}(n)}\widehat{f}_{3}(n).\end{split}

(4.19)

Then,
the trilinear driver associated with the modulated mKdV (
1.14
) is given by

\displaystyle X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{N}(U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{1},U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{2},U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{3})dt^{\prime},

(4.20)

where

U^{w}(t)=e^{-w(t)\partial_{x}^{3}}

and
\mathcal{N}
is as in (
4.19
).
By taking the Fourier transform, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\big(X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\big)(n)&=in\sum_{A_{n}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}})\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2})\widehat{f}_{3}(n_{3})\\
&\quad-3i(t-r)n\widehat{f}_{1}(n)\overline{\widehat{f}_{2}(n)}\widehat{f}_{3}(n)\\
&=:\mathcal{F}\big(X_{t,r}^{(1)}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\big)(n)+\mathcal{F}\big(X_{t,r}^{(2)}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\big)(n),\end{split}

(4.21)

where
\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\cdot)
is as in (
1.6
) and

X^{(1)}
corresponds to the non-resonant part of
X
(namely

\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n})\neq 0
), while

X^{(2)}
corresponds to the resonant part of
X
(namely

\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n})=0
).
Here,
\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n})
denotes the resonance function for mKdV given by

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n})=\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(n,n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})

\displaystyle=-n^{3}+n_{1}^{3}+n_{2}^{3}+n_{3}^{3}.

Recall that, under
n=n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n})

\displaystyle=-3(n-n_{1})(n-n_{2})(n-n_{3}).

(4.22)

As in the case of the modulated KdV studied in the previous subsection,
once we prove the following proposition
on basic mapping properties
of the trilinear driver
X
defined in (
4.20
),
local well-posedness of the modulated mKdV
and other related properties
(Theorem
1.9
and Theorem
1.15
) follow
from Proposition
3.8
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Given
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Then,
the driver
X
defined in (
4.20
)
belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.9
).

(ii)

(persistence of regularity).

Suppose that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Then,
for any
s_{0}>s
,
the driver
X

belongs to

\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.14
).

(iii)

(nonlinear smoothing).

In addition, suppose that
s_{0}>s\geq\frac{1}{2}
satisfies
(
1.26
).
Then,
the driver
X

belongs to

\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.13
).

(iv)

(Galerkin approximation).

Suppose that
s>\frac{1}{2}
.
Then,
the truncated driver
X^{N}
, defined by

\displaystyle X_{t,r}^{N}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{1},\mathbf{P}_{N}U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{2},\mathbf{P}_{N}U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{3})dt^{\prime},

(4.23)

converges
to
X

in
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{3}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
as
N\to\infty
,
where
\mathcal{N}
is as in (
4.19
).

Proof.

Let
\rho,s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
4.1
,
we verify the hypotheses
in Lemma
3.1

with
k=3
.

(i)
We first consider the resonant part
X^{(2)}
.
From (
4.21
),
we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{3}(H^{s})}\lesssim|t-r|

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that

s\geq\frac{1}{2}
, just as in the usual (unmodulated) mKdV case.

Next, we consider the non-resonant part
X^{(1)}
.
From (
4.21
) and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\begin{split}&\|X_{t,r}^{(1)}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2s+2}\bigg|\sum_{A_{n}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n}))\prod_{j=1}^{3}\widehat{f}_{j}(n_{j})\bigg|^{2}\\
&\quad\lesssim\bigg(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{A_{n}}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s+2}}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}\langle n_{3}\rangle^{2s}}|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n}))|^{2}\bigg)\prod_{j=1}^{3}\|f_{j}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}.\end{split}

(4.24)

By symmetry,
assume that
|n_{1}|\gtrsim\max(|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|,|n_{3}|)
,
which implies
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|
.
Then, from (
1.5
)
and (
4.22
) with
s\geq 0
and (
4.18
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|X_{t,r}^{(1)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{3}(H^{s})}&\leq\bigg(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{A_{n}}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2}}{\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}\langle n_{3}\rangle^{2s}}|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n}))|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}\end{aligned}

(4.25)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
where
A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}
is given by

\displaystyle A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}^{2}=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{A_{n}}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2}}{\langle n_{2}+n_{3}\rangle^{2\rho}\prod_{j=2}^{3}\langle n_{j}\rangle^{2s}\langle n-n_{j}\rangle^{2\rho}}.

Fix small
\varepsilon>0
.
From Hölder’s inequality, Young’s inequality (with
\rho\geq\frac{1}{2}
),
and Lemma
2.1
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}^{2}&\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2}\bigg\|\frac{1}{\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}\langle n-n_{2}\rangle^{2\rho}}\bigg\|_{\ell^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{1+2\varepsilon}}_{n_{2}}}^{2}\bigg\|\frac{1}{\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2\rho}}\bigg\|_{\ell^{1+\varepsilon}_{n_{2}}}\\
&\lesssim\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2}\bigg\|\frac{1}{\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}\langle n-n_{2}\rangle^{2\rho}}\bigg\|_{\ell^{\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)}{1+2\varepsilon}}_{n_{2}}}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2-4\min(s,\rho)}<\infty,\end{split}

(4.26)

provided that

\min(s,\rho)\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

(ii)
As for the resonant part
X^{(2)}
, it suffices
to note that

\displaystyle\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s_{0}+2}}{\langle n\rangle^{2s_{0}+4s}}\lesssim 1,

uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
provided that

s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
As for the non-resonant part
X^{(1)}
,
without loss of generality,
assume that
|n_{1}|\gtrsim\max(|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|,|n_{3}|)
,
which implies
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|
.
Then, proceeding as in (
4.24
) and (
4.25
)
with
s\geq 0
(see also (
3.8
)),
we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}^{(1)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{3,1}^{s,s_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{n}\\
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s_{0}+2}}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s_{0}}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}\langle n_{3}\rangle^{2s}}|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}}(\bar{n}))|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that

\min(\rho,s)\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

(iii)
As for the resonant part
X^{(2)}
, it suffices
to note that

\displaystyle\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s_{0}+2}}{\langle n\rangle^{6s}}\lesssim 1,

uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
provided that

s_{0}\leq 3s-1
.
As for the non-resonant part
X^{(1)}
,
without loss of generality,
assume that
|n_{1}|\gtrsim\max(|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|,|n_{3}|)
,
which implies
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|
.
Then,
proceeding as in (
4.24
) and (
4.25
)
with
s_{0}>s\geq 0
,
we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}^{(1)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{3}(H^{s};H^{s_{0}})}

\displaystyle\leq A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}^{(1)}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
where
A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}^{(1)}
is given by

\displaystyle(A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}^{(1)})^{2}=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{A_{n}}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{2s_{0}-2s+2}}{\langle n_{2}+n_{3}\rangle^{2\rho}\prod_{j=2}^{3}\langle n_{j}\rangle^{2s}\langle n-n_{j}\rangle^{2\rho}}.

Here, by a crude estimate,
we have dropped
the factor
\langle n-n_{1}\rangle^{2\rho}
(see Remark
1.16
below).
By proceeding as in (
4.26
) with
Lemma
2.1
,
we have

\displaystyle(A_{\text{\rm mKdV}}^{(1)})^{2}\lesssim\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{2s_{0}-2s+2-4\min(\rho,s)}<\infty,

provided that (
1.26
) holds.

(iv)
From (
4.21
) with (
4.23
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\big(X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\big)(n)-\mathcal{F}\big(X^{N}_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\big)(n)

\displaystyle=in\sum_{\widetilde{A}_{n}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm mKdV}})\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2})\widehat{f}_{3}(n_{3})

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbf{1}_{\{|n|>N\}}\cdot 3i(t-r)n\widehat{f}_{1}(n)\overline{\widehat{f}_{2}(n)}\widehat{f}_{3}(n).

Here,
\widetilde{A}_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{n}=\big\{(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})\in A_{n}:\max(|n|,|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|,|n_{3}|)>N\big\},

where
A_{n}
is as in (
4.18
).
Let
X^{N,(1)}
and
X^{N,(2)}
denote the non-resonant and resonant
parts of
X^{N}
, respectively.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}^{(2)}-X_{t,r}^{N,(2)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{3}(H^{s})}\lesssim N^{-\varepsilon}|t-r|

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that
s\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon
.
Under the same condition on
s
,
by noting
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{A}_{n}}\lesssim N^{-\varepsilon}\max(|n|,|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|,|n_{3}|)^{\varepsilon}
,
a slight modification of (
4.25
) and (
4.26
)
yields

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}^{(1)}-X_{t,r}^{N,(1)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{3}(H^{s})}\lesssim N^{-\varepsilon}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
.
∎

4.3.
Modulated KdV equation on the real line

We conclude this section by
considering
the modulated KdV (
1.2
) on the real line.
The bilinear driver associated with the modulated KdV is
given by
X
in (
4.3
),
where we view all the operators and functions
as those defined on
\mathbb{R}
.
By taking the Fourier transform, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}\big(X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\big)(\xi)=i\xi\intt_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))\widehat{f}_{1}(\xi_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(\xi_{2})d\xi_{1},

(4.27)

where
\Phi^{w}_{t,r}
is as in (
1.6
)
and

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi})

\displaystyle=\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=-\xi^{3}+\xi_{1}^{3}+\xi_{2}^{3}.

As in (
4.6
),
we have

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi})=-3\xi\xi_{1}\xi_{2}

(4.28)

under
\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}
.
Then, as in the previous subsections,
local well-posedness of the modulated KdV (
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{R}

(Theorem
1.7
(i)) follows
from Proposition
3.8

once we prove
the following proposition.

Proposition 4.4
.

Given
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.11
).
Then,
the driver
X
defined in (
4.3
)
belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})

defined in (
2.9
).

(ii)

(persistence of regularity).

Suppose that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.11
).
Then,
for any
s_{0}>s
,
the driver
X

belongs to

\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})

defined in (
2.14
).

Proof.

While we proceed as in the proof of Proposition
4.1

for the modulated KdV on
\mathbb{T}
,
we need to pay particular attention to low frequencies
in the current real line case.

(i)
Proceeding as in (
4.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\xi_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi.

(4.29)

On the other hand, by Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|X_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\bigg(\intt_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi_{1}\bigg)\\
&\quad\times\Big(\intt_{\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{2s}|\widehat{f}_{1}(\xi_{1})|^{2}|\widehat{f}_{2}(\xi_{2})|^{2}d\xi_{1}\Big)d\xi\\
&\leq\bigg(\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi_{1}\bigg)\|f_{1}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\|f_{2}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.\end{split}

(4.30)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi_{1}.

(4.31)

Then,
by dividing the frequency space
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\xi,\xi_{1}}
into the following four regions:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} D_{1}&=\{|\xi|<1,|\xi_{1}|<2\},\\
D_{2}&=\{|\xi|<1,|\xi_{1}|\geq 2\},\\
D_{3}&=\Big\{|\xi|\geq 1,|\xi_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2},|\xi_{2}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big\},\\
D_{4}&=\Big\{|\xi|\geq 1,|\xi_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2},|\xi_{2}|<\frac{1}{2}\Big\}\cup\Big\{|\xi|\geq 1,|\xi_{1}|<\frac{1}{2},|\xi_{2}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big\}\end{aligned}

(4.32)

and applying (
4.29
) and (
4.31
)
(with symmetry
\xi_{1}\leftrightarrow\xi_{2}
on
D_{4}
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\|X_{t,r}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|\xi|<1}\intt_{|\xi_{1}|<2}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{|\xi_{1}|\geq 2}\intt_{|\xi|<1}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{|\xi_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}}\intt_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi|\geq 1\\
|\xi-\xi_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi

(4.33)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1}\intt_{|\xi-\xi_{1}|<\frac{1}{2}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=:A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}+A_{4}.

From (
1.5
) with (
4.28
), we have

\displaystyle A_{1}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}

(4.34)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T

without any restriction on
\rho>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Next, we consider
A_{2}
.
When
s\geq 0
, it follows from the triangle inequality and (
1.5
)
that

\displaystyle A_{2}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}

(4.35)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T

without any restriction on
\rho>0
(and
s\geq 0
).
Now, suppose that
s<0
. Note from (
1.5
) that

\displaystyle|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(a)|\leq\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}\frac{|t-r|^{\gamma}}{\langle a\rangle^{\rho}}\leq\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}\frac{|t-r|^{\gamma}}{\langle a\rangle^{\rho^{\prime}}}

(4.36)

for any
a\in\mathbb{R}

and

0<\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho
.
Under
|\xi|<1
and
|\xi_{1}|\geq 2
,
we have
|\xi-\xi_{1}|>1
.
In particular, we have
|\xi_{1}|\sim\langle\xi_{1}\rangle

and
|\xi-\xi_{1}|\sim\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle
.
Moreover,
we have

\langle\xi\xi_{1}(\xi-\xi_{1})\rangle\geq|\xi\xi_{1}(\xi-\xi_{1})|
.
Then, with (
4.36
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{2}&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}^{2}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\intt_{|\xi|<1}\xi^{2}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi_{1}|\geq 2\\
|\xi-\xi_{1}|>1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\xi_{1}(\xi-\xi_{1})\rangle^{2\rho^{\prime}}\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi\\
&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}^{2}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\intt_{|\xi|<1}|\xi|^{2-2\rho^{\prime}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi_{1}|\geq 2\\
|\xi-\xi_{1}|>1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\xi_{1}(\xi-\xi_{1})|^{2s+2\rho^{\prime}}}d\xi\\
&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}^{2}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\end{split}

(4.37)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that
2-2\rho^{\prime}>-1
and
s+\rho^{\prime}\geq 0
,
which can be satisfied by choosing
suitable
0<\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho

if

\displaystyle\rho>0,\quad s>-\frac{3}{2},\quad\text{and}\quad s\geq-\rho.

(4.38)

As for
A_{3}
,
there is no low frequency issue.
Thus, by
proceeding (
4.9
) and (
4.10
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{3}&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\sup_{|\xi_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}}\intt_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi|\geq 1\\
|\xi-\xi_{1}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\xi\rangle^{2s+2-2\rho}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s+2\rho}}d\xi\\
&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\end{aligned}

(4.39)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that
(
4.11
) holds.

As for
A_{4}
,
we first note that

|\xi_{1}|\sim|\xi|\geq 1

and
\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle\sim 1

under

|\xi|\geq 1
and
|\xi-\xi_{1}|<\frac{1}{2}
.
Then, from (
1.5
), (
4.28
), and
a change of variables
\zeta_{2}=\xi^{2}(\xi-\xi_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{4}&\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1}\xi^{2}\intt_{|\xi-\xi_{1}|<\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\xi^{2}(\xi-\xi_{1})\rangle^{2\rho}}d\xi_{1}\\
&=\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1}\xi^{2}\intt_{|\zeta_{2}|<\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\zeta_{2}\rangle^{2\rho}}d\zeta_{2}\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\end{aligned}

(4.40)

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
, provided that
\rho>\frac{1}{2}

(and any
s\in\mathbb{R}
).

Therefore, putting (
4.33
),
(
4.34
),
(
4.35
),
(
4.37
),
(
4.39
), and (
4.40
)
together, we conclude
from Lemma
3.1
(i)
that

X\in\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})
under (
1.11
).

(ii) Write
X=X^{1}+X^{2}
,
where
X^{1}
is the contribution from
|\xi_{1}|\geq|\xi_{2}|
in (
4.27
)
and
X^{2}=X-X^{1}
.
We only consider
X^{1}
since
X^{2}
can be handled
in an analogous manner.
From (
4.32
)
with (
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|X^{1}_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2,1}^{s,s_{0}}}\leq\widetilde{A}_{1}+\widetilde{A}_{2}+\widetilde{A}_{3}+\widetilde{A}_{4},

where
\widetilde{A}_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,4
,
is obtained from
A_{j}
in (
4.33
)
by replacing

\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}

and
\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}

with
\langle\xi\rangle^{2s_{0}}

and
\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s_{0}}
, respectively.

Suppose that (
1.11
) holds and that
s_{0}>s
.
From
(
4.34
),
a slight modification of (
4.39
)
(as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
(ii)), and
(
4.40
),
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{1}+\widetilde{A}_{3}+\widetilde{A}_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
.
When
s\geq 0
, we also have

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
.
When
s<0
,
a slight modification of (
4.37
) with
|\xi_{1}|\sim|\xi-\xi_{1}|
yields

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}^{2}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\intt_{|\xi|<1}\xi^{2}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\xi_{1}|\geq 2\\
|\xi-\xi_{1}|>1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\xi_{1}(\xi-\xi_{1})\rangle^{2\rho^{\prime}}\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s_{0}}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}^{2}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\intt_{|\xi|<1}|\xi|^{2-2\rho^{\prime}}\sup_{|\xi_{1}|\geq 2}\frac{1}{|\xi_{1}|^{2s_{0}+2s+4\rho^{\prime}}}d\xi

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\rho,\gamma}_{T}}^{2}|t-r|^{2\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
provided that
2-2\rho^{\prime}>-1
and
s_{0}+s+2\rho^{\prime}\geq 0
,
which can be satisfied by choosing
suitable
0<\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho

under (
1.11
) and
s_{0}>s
.
Hence,
from Lemma
3.1
(ii),
we conclude that, if (
1.11
) holds,
then

X\in\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})

for any
s_{0}>s
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

In order to prove analogues of Proposition
4.1
(iii) and (iv)
as a modification of the proof of Proposition
4.4
,
we would need to
(at least) bound

\displaystyle A_{4}^{\prime}=\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1}\intt_{|\xi-\xi_{1}|<\frac{1}{2}}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s+\varepsilon)}\xi^{2}\frac{|\Phi^{w}_{t,r}(\Xi_{\text{\rm KdV}}(\bar{\xi}))|^{2}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle\xi-\xi_{1}\rangle^{2s}}d\xi_{1}

for some
\varepsilon>0
.
By proceeding as in (
4.40
), we have

\displaystyle A_{4}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1}|\xi|^{2+2\varepsilon}\intt_{|\xi-\xi_{1}|<\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\xi^{2}(\xi-\xi_{1})\rangle^{2\rho}}d\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\Phi^{w}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{2\gamma}\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1}|\xi|^{2\varepsilon}\intt_{|\xi_{2}|<\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{2\rho}}d\xi_{2}=\infty.

This shows that
we can not establish nonlinear smoothing or convergence
of the Galerkin approximation
as a straightforward modification of the proof of Proposition
4.4
.

5.
Modulated BO and ILW equations on the circle

In this section, we consider the
modulated BO and (scaled) ILW equations
on the circle.
By establishing regularity properties
of the associated drivers, we first prove
their local well-posedness and related properties
(Theorems
1.11
and
1.17
)
in Subsections
5.1
and

5.2
,
where the claims for the modulated (scaled) ILW follow
from a perturbation of the BO case.
This perturbative viewpoint
allows us to
easily prove
deep-water convergence (Theorem
1.13
(i)) in Subsection
5.3
.
On the other hand,
careful analysis
on the resonance function
\widetilde{\Xi}_{\delta}(\bar{n})

for the scaled ILW defined in (
5.41
)
is required to establish
shallow-water convergence
(Theorem
1.13
(ii));
see Subsection
5.4
.

5.1.
Modulated BO equation

The bilinear driver associated with
the modulated BO (
1.16
) on
\mathbb{T}
is given by

\displaystyle X^{\text{\rm BO}}_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{r}^{t}U^{w}(t^{\prime})^{-1}\partial_{x}\big((U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{1})(U^{w}(t^{\prime})f_{2})\big)dt^{\prime},

(5.1)

where

\displaystyle U^{w}(t)=e^{w(t)\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}}

(5.2)

denotes
the modulated linear propagator
for (
1.16
).
By taking the Fourier transform, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big(X^{\text{\rm BO}}_{t,r}(f_{1},f_{2})\big)(n)=in\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\Phi_{t,r}^{w}(\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n}))\widehat{f}_{1}(n_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(n_{2})

(5.3)

where
\Phi_{t,r}^{w}
is as in (
1.6
)
and

\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})
denotes the resonance function for BO given by

\displaystyle\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})=\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(n,n_{1},n_{2})=-|n|n+|n_{1}|n_{1}+|n_{2}|n_{2}.

(5.4)

As in the previous section,
Theorem
1.11
(i) and Theorem
1.17
(i) and (ii) follow
from Proposition
3.8
,
once we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

Given
\rho\geq 1
,
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
, and
T>0
,
let
w
be
(\rho,\gamma)
-irregular on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

(i)

Suppose that
\rho\geq 1
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.22
).
Then,
the driver
X^{\text{\rm BO}}
defined in (
5.1
)
belongs to
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.9
).

(ii)

(persistence of regularity).

Suppose that
\rho\geq 1
and
s\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
(
1.22
).
Then,
for any
s_{0}>s
,
the driver
X^{\text{\rm BO}}

belongs to

\mathcal{Y}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.14
).

(iii)

(nonlinear smoothing).

In addition, suppose that
s_{0}>s
satisfies
(
1.27
).
Then,
the driver
X^{\text{\rm BO}}

belongs to

\mathcal{X}^{s,s_{0},\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

defined in (
2.13
).

(iv)

(Galerkin approximation).

Define the truncated driver
X^{\text{\rm BO},N}

as in (
4.7
),
where we replace

U^{w}(t)
by the modulated linear propagator
for BO defined in (
5.2
).
Suppose that
\rho>1
and
s>-\frac{1}{2}\rho
.
Then,
X^{\text{\rm BO},N}

converges
to
X^{\text{\rm BO}}

in
\mathcal{X}^{s,\gamma}_{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
4.1
,
we verify the hypotheses
in Lemma
3.1

with
k=2
.

(i)
Proceeding as in (
4.8
) (see also (
4.9
)),
we have

\displaystyle\|X^{\text{\rm BO}}_{t,r}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(H^{s})}

\displaystyle\lesssim A_{\text{\rm BO}}\|\Phi^{w}\|_{\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho,\gamma}}|t-r|^{\gamma}

for any
0\leq r<t\leq T
,
where
A_{\text{\rm BO}}
is given by

\displaystyle A_{\text{\rm BO}}=\bigg(\sup_{n_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1}=n-n_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle n\rangle^{2s+2}\frac{1}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{2s}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{2s}|\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})|^{2\rho}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

From (
5.4
), we have, under
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
,

(a)

If
n_{1}n_{2}>0
(i.e.
n
,
n_{1}
, and
n_{2}
all have the same sign), then
|\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})|=2|n_{1}n_{2}|
.

(b)

If
n,n_{1}>0>n_{2}
, then
|\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})|=2|nn_{2}|
.

(c)

If
n,n_{2}>0>n_{1}
, then
|\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})|=2|nn_{1}|
.

(d)

If
n_{1}>0>n,n_{2}
, then
|\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})|=2|nn_{1}|
.

(e)

If
n_{2}>0>n,n_{1}
, then
|\Xi_{\text{\rm BO}}(\bar{n})|=2|nn_{2}|
.

By symmetry,
we only consider Cases (a) and (b) in the following.

\bullet

Case (i.a):

n_{1}n_{2}>0
.

Without loss of generality, assume that
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n_{2}|
.
Then, we have
|n_{1}|\gtrsim|n|
under
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
.
In view of Lemma
2.1
, we have
